### [1] [A $B_{1g}$ phonon unlocks $d$-wave superconductivity in the repulsive Hubbard model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37746)
*Wen O. Wang,Tianshu Li,Xiaoyue Ma,Rong Zhang,Lei Zhang,Shruti Agarwal,Benjamin Cohen-Stead,Emily Z. Zhang,Brian Moritz,Steven Johnston,Zhi-Xun Shen,Thomas P. Devereaux*

Abstract: Using numerically exact finite-temperature quantum Monte Carlo, we demonstrate that a $B_{1g}$ phonon coupled to the doped $t$-$t'$ repulsive Hubbard model produces genuine $d$-wave superconductivity at a substantial transition temperature, in excess of either interaction alone. The observed trends suggest that stronger Hubbard repulsion may cooperate with the phonon to further raise the pairing scale. The enhancement persists at stronger repulsion within the accessible window, limited by the fermion sign problem, and follows a common trend across phonon frequencies $Ω$ when organized by a retardation scale $2πT/Ω$. We also find a well-defined superconducting dome in doping, echoing a characteristic feature of correlated superconductors. Our results identify the $B_{1g}$ phonon as a plausible microscopic ingredient for robust $d$-wave superconductivity in the Hubbard model and suggest a concrete, physically motivated route toward materials with higher superconducting transition temperatures.

</details>

Abstract: Phonon bursts from ionizing radiation cause correlated errors in superconducting quantum processors. We study prescribed nonequilibrium phonon pulses in a Majorana qubit formed by two $2\,μ\mathrm{m}$ proximitized nanowires. Combining the lattice Lutchyn--Oreg model, microscopic electron--phonon coupling, and parity-resolved many-body dynamics, we track excitation, relaxation, and logical coherence. Without quasiparticle escape, recombination restores the initial Majorana occupations in about 130 ns, while phonons exchanged during excitation and recovery can retain logical-state information, leaving permanent coherence loss. For mirror-symmetric wires, the fractional coherence loss per wire is 0.75--1.0 times the excitation probability averaged over the two logical states, even at zero Majorana splitting; breaking mirror symmetry can suppress dephasing at small splitting. Factorized Boltzmann kinetics neglects quasiparticle correlations and overestimates residual parity flips. At equal deposited energy density, phonon spectra yield different excitation probabilities and coherence losses. Comparisons with Kitaev chains matched to the wire's gap and Majorana decay length reveal that low electron density suppresses excitation by energetic phonons, whereas hard-wall boundaries in a high-density control increase excitation efficiency by a factor of $5\times10^4$ relative to the wire for pulses centered at 1 meV. Magnetic-field and chemical-potential tuning yield different phonon sensitivities at the same bulk gap through changes in spin and electron--hole structure. Including hybrid states' semiconductor weights reduces near-threshold excitation rates fourfold when the tunnel coupling equals the parent superconducting gap. Our framework connects phonon spectra to leakage, poisoning, and coherence loss to guide Majorana-device design.

</details>

Abstract: TiN thin films have received great attention in the field of superconducting quantum computing due to their low loss characteristics derived from stable chemical and thermal properties, as well as excellent electrical conductivity. In this study, TiN thin films with a thickness of 35 nm were epitaxially grown on MgO substrates using the DC reactive magnetron sputtering technique. The crystal structure, film thickness, and surface morphology of the thin films were characterized. The films exhibit a very flat surface with a root mean square roughness of only a few tenths of a nanometer. The influences of nitrogen ($N_2$) flow rate on the surface morphology, superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$), residual resistivity ratio (RRR), normal-state resistivity ($ρ_n$), upper critical field, and superconducting dimensionality were investigated systematically. The experimental results show that the TiN film deposited with a $N_2$ flow rate of 1.5 sccm exhibits optimal performance, including the highest $T_c$ (4.93 K), the largest RRR, the lowest $ρ_n$, and the lowest slope of the out-of-film upper critical field near Tc. Moreover, it is found that the background vacuum inside the growth chamber has a significant impact on the superconducting properties of TiN films, and the degradation of vacuum degree can lead to the decrease of $T_c$. The upper critical field reveals a significant anisotropic feature. Importantly, the in-film upper critical field data demonstrates that the optimization of sample quality leads to deviation from two-dimensional superconducting behavior, which provides a new perspective on the influencing factors of superconducting dimensionality beyond the thickness of films. The present results provide useful references for a comprehensive understanding of the relationship between growth parameters and the superconducting properties of thin films.

</details>

Abstract: Phase-sensitive experiments based on Josephson tunneling have been considered as a pivotal method to determine the pairing symmetry in high-temperature (high-Tc) cuprate superconductors. However, in iron-based superconductors, traditional phase-sensitive experiments face setbacks because of the complexity of pairing symmetry in multiband systems. Here, using scanning Josephson tunneling microscopy (SJTM), we report the half-unit-cell periodic Josephson tunneling modulations in monolayer high-Tc Fe(Te,Se) film epitaxially grown on SrTiO3(001). The Josephson tunneling almost vanishes along the lines connecting next-nearest-neighbor Fe atoms under an s-wave superconducting tip. Combined with single-particle tunneling spectra showing the full superconducting gap, our experimental results are consistent with the sign-reversal nodeless d-wave pairing symmetry. Our findings provide sub-unit-cell scale superconducting phase information for understanding the mechanism of high-Tc superconductivity. We anticipate our work as a starting point for atomically resolved Josephson interference experiments using SJTM in various unconventional superconductors.

</details>

Abstract: The superconducting diode effect is often associated with finite-momentum pairing, although model studies indicate that rectification reflects the asymmetry of the free energy about its minimum rather than the equilibrium pair momentum itself. Using a Wannier-based, self-consistent finite-$\mathbf Q$ Bogoliubov-de Gennes approach with a Fermi-surface-adaptive quadrature that converges the critical currents, we show that the layer parity and orientation of magnetic proximity select the diode response of monolayer NbSe$_2$. For in-plane exchange, the equilibrium pair momentum $\mathbf Q_{\rm eq}$ and the diode efficiency are both linear in the layer-odd exchange contrast: parallel proximity on the two Se sublayers is reciprocal, and antiparallel proximity doubles the response. An out-of-plane component separates the two observables. It leaves $\mathbf Q_{\rm eq}$ nearly unchanged but, through pair breaking, suppresses the efficiency by more than an order of magnitude and, at low temperature, reverses it. Purely out-of-plane exchange, for which $C_3$ symmetry forbids a helical term, keeps $\mathbf Q_{\rm eq}=0$ yet produces a trigonal diode effect, controlled by the layer-even exchange and larger than the in-plane one above $0.65$~meV at low temperature. Magnetic layer parity thus selects between a helical and a trigonal diode channel. The trigonal channel is an intrinsic, symmetry-allowed contribution to the field-free rectification reported in NbSe$_2$/CrBr$_3$ and predicts that two-sided out-of-plane proximity cancels it when antiparallel and, at the same exchange per layer, enhances it about fourfold when parallel, in the computed range.

</details>

Abstract: Identifying the key ingredients for superconductivity under pressure in the Ruddlesden-Popper nickelates remains a formidable challenge, due to a complex interplay between structural variations, oxygen content and location, electronically ordered phases, and the need for high pressures to reach the superconducting phase. To disentangle these complex inter-dependencies, here we study a series of clean single crystals of trilayer La$_4$Ni$_3$O$_{10}$. We discover that enhanced biaxial, compressive pressures induce a zero-resistance state at a 60\% lower pressure compared to previous reports. At the same time, we completely separate the low-pressure density wave phase from superconductivity, effectively ruling out that soft, quantum critical fluctuations of the density wave order parameter act as the pairing glue in this system. Our results establish biaxial, in-plane pressures as a powerful tool for promoting superconductivity in nickelates, unifying the required pressure scales for superconductivity in different Ruddlesden-Popper structures, and providing a connection to the recently discovered thin film samples.

Abstract: We study the local Josephson response near nonmagnetic impurities and vortices in a self-consistent two-orbital $s_{\pm}$ superconductor. For a local $s$-wave tip that couples equally to the two orbitals, anomalous spectral contributions of opposite sign nearly cancel in the clean sample. Nonmagnetic impurities redistribute these contributions and can reverse the local coupling without a corresponding $π$ phase shift of the bond pair fields. Negative coupling appears at off-center sites at weaker impurity potentials than at the impurity site. The impurity-induced change is concentrated at lower quasiparticle energies, while states over a broader energy range contribute to the total clean and impurity couplings. In zero field, the coupling also changes sign as the tip gap is varied, and increasing the tunneling strength can reverse the $m=1$ sine component of the current without changing the sample state. At the chosen coherence-peak energy, increasing impurity strength mainly changes the relative intensities of existing quasiparticle-interference features. In a self-consistent two-vortex state, the Josephson response develops a spatially varying phase relative to the condensate. The response and condensate share the winding close to each core, while larger loops enclose additional winding of the Josephson response. The local Josephson sign and phase reflect the anomalous spectrum and the tip--sample coupling.

</details>

Abstract: Superconducting diode effects are characterized by direction-dependent critical currents in the superconducting state. Here, we investigate diode-like transport in 128-nm-thick Mo thin film deposited on Si_SiO2 substrate by DC magnetron sputtering. The thin film exhibit a superconducting transition temperature TC of 5.8 K. Pronounced current-direction asymmetry and hysteresis are observed in current-voltage characteristics measured in a closed-cycle refrigerator. In contrast, the same thin film exhibits a substantially more symmetric response in a 14T cryogenic system with improved sample thermalization. We attribute this strong dependence on the measurement environment to electrothermal effects arising from current-induced Joule heating and finite thermal relaxation. As the current approaches the superconducting-to-resistive switching condition, the emergence of a resistive component leads to a rapid increase in Joule heating, raising the sample temperature and modifying the switching condition. Conversely, during the resistive-to-superconducting transition, residual heating and finite thermal relaxation reduce the switching current. Since P is equal to I2R it is independent of current polarity, the observed asymmetry is not associated with polarity-dependent Joule heating but with the interplay of thermal relaxation, sweep history, and thermal boundary conditions. The suppression of the asymmetry under improved thermalization, together with similar behavior observed in NbN reference films, indicates that the apparent diode-like response is strongly influenced by electrothermal effects. These results highlight the need to distinguish thermal switching and hysteresis from intrinsic nonreciprocal superconductivity when identifying superconducting diode effects in transport measurements.

</details>

Abstract: Majorana zero modes (MZMs) are promising for topological quantum computation, with vortices in topological superconductors serving as a leading platform. Recent experiments show that integer Abrikosov vortices in iron-based superconductors can split into fractional vortices (FVs) carrying fractional flux quanta. Abstract: Disorder plays a central role in correlated quantum materials, but its effects can be highly selective when several electronic orders coexist. The van der Waals heterostructure 4Hb-TaS2 provides such a case, combining superconductivity, charge-order tendencies, strong scattering, and magnetic memory manifested through spontaneous vortices. Here we use Se substitution as a controlled local perturbation of the disorder landscape in 4Hb-TaS2. We find that superconductivity remains remarkably robust while the magnetic memory is strongly suppressed at higher Se concentrations. These results show that Se substitution does not create the hidden magnetic memory, but instead selectively weakens its macroscopic manifestation. More broadly, they reveal a sharp separation between robust superconducting and hidden-memory phases, and a highly disorder-sensitive spontaneous-vortex response in 4Hb-TaS2.

</details>

Abstract: Understanding the normal state of unconventional superconductors is central to uncovering the origin of superconductivity itself. Unconventional superconductivity often emerges from correlated electronic states characterized by intertwined symmetry-breaking orders. A striking example is 4Hb-TaS2, whose superconducting state exhibits a remarkable range of phenomena, including an unusual magnetic memory effect. Previous experiments showed that magnetic memory can be encoded above Tc and later revealed through spontaneous vortices below Tc, suggesting time-reversal symmetry breaking. However, since memory and vorticity are intertwined, this prevents establishing whether the effect results from trapped flux, weak conventional magnetism, or a true hidden electronic order. Here, we show that magnetic memory persists in the vortex-free Meissner regime of exfoliated 4Hb-TaS2 flakes, where it appears as a stable remanent modification of the superconducting screening response. Our results rule out explanations based on trapped vortices. Furthermore, the spatial profile of the flux generated by trained samples at zero field is inconsistent with conventional weak magnetism, suggesting a hidden electronic phase that imprints an effective flux on the superconducting condensate.

</details>

Abstract: We study a spinless $π$-flux lattice superconductor whose normal-state momentum-space fundamental domain is a Klein bottle. Within this model, a glide lift and a hidden antiunitary symmetry independently force the Bogoliubov--de Gennes (BdG) Chern number to vanish. A diagonal pairing breaks the antiunitary obstruction exactly when its real part is nonzero; this is necessary, not sufficient, for a chiral phase. We derive explicit Pfaffians determining Chern parity and identify three analytic families of gap closings in a real-pairing section. For a chiral state on a closed real-space Klein bottle, the prescribed pairing and gluing produce a $ΔC=2$ chirality wall. A translation-invariant unfolding resolves two co-propagating Majorana branches. Their finite-size levels depend on circumference and holonomy; the near-zero pair is lifted at first order by allowed local seam perturbations. A cut-cylinder control has the same low-energy channel count, so gluing reconnects existing gapless content. We also distinguish literal gluing from deck projection: particle--hole symmetry exchanges the deck eigenblocks, precluding their interpretation as independent BdG quotient Hamiltonians. The contribution is a model-specific account of pairing compatibility and closed-surface seam spectra. The energy cost is evaluated for a fixed pairing background; neither a protected zero mode nor a self-consistent wall solution is claimed.

</details>

Abstract: We solve the Klein-Gordon oscillator in a non-commutative phase space, generalized using Dunkl derivatives, both with and without interaction with a uniform magnetic field. For each of these cases, we begin by introducing the Dunkl derivative into the standard commutative phase space to evaluate the reflection parity symmetries. Then, using the Seiberg-Witten map, we extend the generalized system to a non-commutative phase space. In both cases, we determine the eigenfunctions and the energy spectrum exactly (which correspond to generalized Landau levels when a magnetic field is present). Furthermore, we compute the non-relativistic limit of the system and graphically analyze the behavior of the energy spectra. Our results demonstrate that the combination of the Dunkl deformation parameters, the non-commutative phase space geometry, and the magnetic field produces a parity-dependent modification of the energy levels. Finally, we verify that when the external magnetic field or the non-commutative parameters vanish, our exact solutions correctly reduce to the commutative Dunkl models established in previous works.

</details>

Abstract: Algebraic quantum field theory describes a quantum system through its observables, their localisation in spacetime, and the states that assign them expectation values. The separation of the specification of observables from the choice of a state makes it possible to compare vacuum, thermal and charged representations without assuming that they share a preferred Hilbert space. We develop the framework from familiar quantum mechanics, construct the free scalar field, and explain how causality and positive energy constrain local measurements. The Reeh--Schlieder theorem, the structure of local von Neumann algebras and the split property reveal key differences between quantum theory in the continuum (i.e. QFT) and ordinary quantum mechanics with finitely many degrees of freedom. Superselection theory relates observable charges to statistics and internal symmetries. Finally, local covariance and perturbative and homological methods extend the discussion to curved spacetime, interactions and gauge theories. We also discuss measurement theory and quantum information aspects of AQFT.

</details>

Abstract: For a charged particle, a spacetime symmetry need preserve the electromagnetic potential only up to a gauge transformation. Starting from the standard Noether--Bessel-Hagen construction, we make explicit the reduction from the relativistic gauge-invariant worldline charge to the generalized constants of nonrelativistic velocity-dependent mechanics, retaining the quasi-invariance boundary term throughout. With the convention $S_p=-mc\int ds+q\int A$, the canonical worldline one-form is $P=-m u^\flat+qA$, and a Killing vector satisfying $\mathcal L_XA=dχ_X$ generates $C_X=P(X)-qχ_X$. Its low-velocity limit is the Noether--Bessel-Hagen charge of the coordinate-time Lagrangian, from which generalized linear momentum, angular momentum and energy follow generator by generator. A uniform magnetic field gives an elementary gauge-dependent representative but gauge-invariant translational charge. The Darwin interaction then supplies the conservative $O(c^{-2})$ many-particle realization after field elimination, while a closed particle--field system is governed by the total stress-energy Killing charge. The result is an explicit reduction map linking three established descriptions without identifying their distinct conserved quantities.

</details>

Abstract: A topological Anderson insulator is an insulator whose non-trivial topological phase is induced by disorder. We develop a homogenization theory of this phenomenon for constant-coefficient elliptic systems perturbed by fluctuations oscillating at scale $\varepsilon$. Periodic fluctuations of every order below that of the operator, with appropriate amplitudes, do not average out: they produce an order-one correction $M$ given by a second-order (Born) formula, and the operator converges in the norm-resolvent sense to its homogenized limit. In particular, electric potentials and magnetic fields of zero mean, usually regarded as topologically trivial perturbations, generate corrections $M$ that change the topological phase. Fluctuations confined to a half-plane create an interface that carries the bulk-difference invariant of the homogenized operator for all $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, a property that is stable under local defects. For random fluctuations, rare regions typically fill the homogenized gap, which becomes a pseudo-gap, and the standard interface index need no longer be defined. We define an index along cuts deformed so as to avoid the rare regions, and prove that it equals almost surely the bulk-difference invariant of the operator homogenized in the Born approximation, for first-order alloys with vanishing low moments and for a class of second-order systems with scalar disorder at criticality that includes the BHZ model.

</details>

Abstract: We develop a rigorous mathematical framework for the nonequilibrium thermodynamics of semilinear stochastic partial differential equations. The invariant Gaussian measure of the associated Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, which need not be time-reversible, serves as the reference measure. We define thermodynamic quantities in terms of probability densities and derive the entropy balance equation. We obtain explicit expressions for the entropy production rate and prove that its vanishing in the stationary state is equivalent to time reversibility. Sample-path entropy production is identified with the logarithm of the Radon-Nikodym derivative of the forward path measure with respect to the time-reversed path measure and is shown to satisfy fluctuation theorems. For nonstationary systems, sample-path entropy production decomposes into housekeeping heat and free-energy dissipation, each satisfying an integral fluctuation theorem. Both have nonnegative expectations, and the mean free-energy dissipation equals the decrease in relative entropy with respect to the stationary distribution. The theory is applied to semilinear stochastic heat equations with spatially correlated noise, a random polymer model, a nonlinear model with a divergence-free drift, and two coupled stochastic heat equations in contact with heat baths at different temperatures.

</details>

Abstract: We study a translation-invariant BCS functional of superconductivity for a system of spin-$1/2$ fermions with spin-dependent chemical potentials, $μ_+\neqμ_-$. Numerical work of Caldas and Mota and analytical work of Freiji, Hainzl, and Seiringer has suggested an unusual first-order phase transition in spin-imbalanced BCS models, in which the normal state becomes globally unstable while remaining locally stable. Here, we prove that a first-order phase transition occurs in such spin-imbalanced BCS functionals. For each sufficiently small fixed spin imbalance and sufficiently low temperature, there is a coexistence coupling $λ_{\mathrm{fo}}$ strictly below the linear-stability threshold $λ_{\mathrm{lin}}$ of the normal state at which the Cooper pair density exhibits a nonzero jump and the minimized energy has a kink. The methodological insight is a coercivity estimate obtained by decomposing the BCS energy minimization into two parts, where the ``inner'' problem minimizes over the one-particle density matrices at fixed pairing and can be exactly solved by a Legendre--Fenchel transform.

Abstract: We study an optimization inverse spectral problem (OISP) for a one-dimensional Sturm--Liouville operator on a finite interval. The problem is to find a potential $V \in L^p(0,1)$ that realizes a prescribed finite set $λ_1^* < λ_2^* < \dots < λ_m^*$ and has the smallest $L^p(0,1)$-distance from an a priori given potential $V_0 \in L^p(0,1)$.
  We formulate and prove results on the regularity of the isospectral manifold, the existence of an OISP solution, and an explicit analytic representation of the optimal potential by means of a power map applied to a linear combination of squared eigenfunctions.
  Using a unified differential-algebraic approach to squared eigenfunctions, we show that whenever these squares are linearly dependent, the corresponding potential can be expressed in terms of them and is an analytic function.
  For $p=2$, we prove that nondegenerate stationary points are isolated and that the number of global minima is finite provided that each of them is nondegenerate. For every $1<p<\infty$, we establish uniqueness for the first eigenvalue when $λ_1^*\geλ_1(V_0)$ and generic global uniqueness in the sense of Baire category in $L^p(0,1)$.
  A separate section provides examples with at least two global minima for $m=1,2$ in the presence of a sufficiently high finite symmetric barrier. We describe an iteration with frozen eigenfunctions and a local Newton--KKT method with continuation in the spectral data, and state explicitly the limits of their guarantees.
  The final part discusses applications of OISP theory to nonlinear boundary value problems and to applied problems of diagnostics and optimal spectral design of acoustic and electromagnetic resonators.

</details>

Abstract: We prove that weak long-range many-body interactions preserve the bulk gap of a free fermion system and that a quasi-local spectral flow transports the free ground state to ground states of the interacting system. Let $Φ_h$ be a free fermionic interaction, possibly including pairing terms, whose infinite-volume Majorana coefficient operator $h$ has no spectrum in $(-g,g)$, and let $\mathcal V$ be an even self-adjoint many-body interaction. For sufficiently small $|λ|$, we construct pure ground states $ω_λ$ of the interacting bulk system $Φ_λ=Φ_h+λ\mathcal V$ and quasi-local automorphisms $β_{0,λ}$ such that $ω_λ=ω_0\circβ_{0,λ}$, where $ω_0$ is the free ground state of $Φ_h$. Each $ω_λ$ has a GNS Hamiltonian with gap at least $g-C_*|λ| \|\mathcal V\|_*$, where $\|\cdot\|_*$ is the relevant interaction norm.
  Our result allows polynomial decay of both the free coefficients and the many-body interaction. For two-site interactions on a uniformly discrete point set $Γ\subset\mathbb{R}^d$, a sufficient condition is decay as $(1+|x-y|)^{-\varpi}$ with $\varpi>2d$, for both the hopping and pairing coefficients and the interaction terms. The spectral-flow generator preserves the spatial exponent of the polynomial interaction norm, and its automorphisms satisfy locality bounds at that exponent. If $h$ and $\mathcal V$ have stretched-exponential decay, so does the spectral-flow generator.
  The proof constructs the spectral flow and establishes the gap through an excitation-number comparison, using polynomial propagation bounds for evolved interactions proved in a companion paper.

</details>

Abstract: We consider bulk-gapped fermion systems whose infinite-volume bulk is obtained by adding sufficiently weak even many-body interactions to a free Hamiltonian with spectral gap $g>0$, allowing polynomial spatial decay of both parts. We prove quantitative local determination of low-energy states: whenever another interaction agrees with the bulk interaction inside a finite or infinite region $U$, with bulk terms crossing its boundary unchanged or removed, all its states with excitation energies in $[0,3g/4]$ have local expectations close to those of the bulk ground state deep inside $U$. The error decays with the distance to the complement of $U$, independently of the interaction terms supported outside $U$ and of the chosen low-energy state.
  Quantitative local determination has strong consequences. (i) The bulk interaction has a unique algebraic ground state. (ii) A strong "local perturbations perturb locally" (LPPL) principle holds: arbitrary distant defects change local ground-state expectations only by a spatially decaying error. (iii) Low-energy excitations in finite samples remain localized near the boundary for all times, up to spatially decaying tails, uniformly over arbitrary even boundary terms. (iv) Correlations from deep inside $U$ to outside $U$ are small, with quantitative bounds in terms of spatial separation and distance to $U^c$.
  To prove local determination of low-energy states, we combine quasi-adiabatic transport of the free ground state to the interacting bulk ground state with a new condition, local excitation detectability (LED). A companion paper establishes gap stability and constructs the transport for weak interactions, while we prove LED at sufficiently weak coupling. The abstract theorem is nonperturbative and applies whenever the transport and LED hypotheses hold.

</details>

Abstract: Twinor theory is formulated as a spacetime-based counterpart to twistor theory using holomorphic reductive Cartan geometry modelled on complexified $(A)dS_4$. The fundamental spin-tractor bundle defines Dirac twinors, its symmetric powers define ladder twinors, and their extremal Lorentz components define chiral twinors. The general \textit{twinor field equation} is $D\mathbf Y=\mathbf J$. Its adjacent contractions and mixed divergence are constrained by source relations; no additional equation is imposed on its completely symmetric projection. On a flat torsion-free Lorentzian background, the adjacent equations with the mass-normalization source and vanishing additional contracted sources, together with vanishing mixed divergence, reproduce the massive \textit{Dirac--Fierz--Pauli system}; the massless chiral realization gives the zero-rest-mass equations. Within the fixed mass normalization, the reality condition yielding a positive real mass squared is the \textit{anti-de Sitter reality condition} on the particle scale, independently of the cosmological scale and background curvature. The mass-coupling phase is therefore present in the classical connection before quantization. Nonlinear gravity is formulated through the full adjoint curvature, with energy--momentum and spin currents specifying Einstein--Cartan dynamics without requiring an action. The adjoint Bianchi identity constrains source compatibility, while a BGG-type sequence organizes torsion-free infinitesimal deformations. Matter interactions enter through the coupled connection and contracted source relations, with curvature compatibility recorded by $D\mathbf J=\mathcal F_D\mathbf Y$. Both chiralities are retained, and the additional consistency requirements for interacting higher-spin fields are distinguished from the underlying bundle construction.

</details>

Abstract: The celebrated Evans--Searles, respectively Gallavotti--Cohen, fluctuation theorem concerns certain universal statistical features of the entropy production rate of a classical system in a transient, respectively steady, state. In this paper, we review and compare several possible extensions of these fluctuation theorems to quantum systems. We present, in an introductory way, the approach proposed in a recent series of works [Lett. Math. Phys. 114, 32 (2024): doi:10.1007/s11005-024-01777-0], [Rev. Math. Phys 38, 2461006 (2026): doi:10.1142/S0129055X24610063], [Commun. Math. Phys. 406, 201 (2025): 10.1007/s00220-025-05360-z] and [J. Math. Phys. 67, 022101 (2026): doi:10.1063/5.0311182] including a previously missing extension of the Gallavotti--Cohen (steady state) version of the FT to two-time measurement entropy production in the algebraic setting of quantum dynamical systems. We focus on the open quantum systems, where a small system interacts with several (infinitely extended) reservoirs, and discuss the following approaches: naive, two-time measurement, phase space contraction and ancilla state tomography. We review the connection with modular theory. The latter provides a way to extend the classical notions to the quantum domain. We discuss the quantum version of the principle of regular entropic fluctuations, introduced in the classical context in [Nonlinearity 24, 699 (2011): doi:10.1088/0951-7715/24/3/003]. Finally, we relate the statistical properties of these various notions of entropy production to spectral resonances of quantum transfer operators and apply this approach to a paradigmatic model of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics: the spin-fermion model. The results presented here shed a new light on the nature of entropic fluctuations in quantum statistical mechanics.

</details>

Abstract: Ordinary $0$-form symmetries $G$ can mix with $1$-form symmetries $Π_2$ to form a $2$-group $\mathcal{G}$. We give a concrete lattice definition for a class of finite $2$-group symmetries in terms of local relations among symmetry operators. These relations identify the Postnikov class $[ρ]\in H^3(BG,Π_2)$ that characterizes the mixing. We develop a method to extract anomalies of these symmetries in one spatial dimension by restricting symmetry operations to an interval and comparing their composition near its endpoints. We also construct explicit qubit symmetry actions in $d=1,2,3$ spatial dimensions for a $2$-group with $G=Π_2=\mathbb{Z}_2$. These actions share the same nontrivial Postnikov class but include both anomaly-free and anomalous realizations. Their cohomological anomalies are classified by $H^{d+2}(B\mathcal G,U(1))$, which is $\mathbb{Z}_2$ for $d=1,2$ and $\mathbb{Z}_2\times\mathbb{Z}_2$ for $d=3$, and our constructions realize every class in these dimensions. Our results connect higher-group symmetry and its anomalies to concrete lattice operations, providing a microscopic framework for studying anomalous symmetries.

Abstract: Preparing complex quantum states of light for quantum information processing tasks often proceeds by converting from simpler states via experimentally accessible operations. We provide fundamental bounds on such probabilistic conversions, both exact and approximate. To do so, we establish a theory of weak, fully relative majorization that compares the disorder of phase-space functions. Our framework is versatile and can be tailored to different systems and sets of allowed operations. Focusing on Wigner functions in multimode photonic systems, we establish rigorous no-go results and tradeoff relations for exact and approximate conversions involving squeezed states, cat states, Gottesman--Kitaev--Preskill states, and Fock states. In doing so, we derive novel bounds and recover known tight ones. Our results demonstrate that phase-space disorder imposes strong limitations on photonic state preparation.

Abstract: Grover's algorithm is a textbook example of quantum advantage, providing a quadratic query speedup over classical methods for unstructured search. In this Letter, we characterize the non-stabilizerness, or magic, developed throughout Grover's protocol, quantifying quantum complexity with respect to the efficiently simulable stabilizer states. First, we compute the compilation-independent magic at the end of each Grover iteration, deriving an analytic prediction for the stabilizer Rényi entropy and proving that it remains intensive as the system size grows. We then investigate the magic overhead due to decomposing the circuit into elementary gates. When the solution to the search problem is known, we show that an efficient decomposition exists that keeps magic bounded despite using an extensive number of Toffoli gates. In contrast, agnostic implementations are expected to generate volume-law non-stabilizerness, as we confirm for the 3-SAT problem, which rules out efficient simulation by stabilizer-based methods in practically relevant cases. Our findings clarify the growth of magic in Grover's algorithm and the role of solution-awareness in its circuit compilation.

</details>

Abstract: The characterization of possible transformations between quantum states and the quantification of entanglement are central for understanding quantum resources. Explicit results are, however, scarce since they require complicated optimizations over transformation protocols or state decompositions. We introduce a method to optimize functions of the entanglement spectrum of bipartite states over mixed-state decompositions with arbitrary accuracy. Our results are based on a convergent semidefinite programming hierarchy of optimizations over entanglement spectra using a natural polynomial basis and Schur-Weyl duality. Our approach can also be used to determine non-polynomial measures, such as the entanglement of formation, and to characterize the dimensionality of entanglement. Moreover, extracting the decomposition allows us to formulate criteria to determine state transformations under local operations and classical communication (LOCC). In particular, we provide a hierarchy of certificates of LOCC non-convertibility from pure to mixed states in any dimension and a heuristic approach for LOCC convertibility.

Abstract: Covariant integral quantization is developed on the double discrete cylinder phase space, with the torus as configuration space, and yields the Wigner distribution and the stellar representation. This phase space is particularly well suited to translationally invariant states, such as the Gottesman Kitaev Preskill (GKP) states. The stellar representations of ideal GKP logical states, which behave as phase eigenstates of the double discrete cylinder, have no zeros on this phase space, whereas those of their superpositions have infinitely many. The vacuum, although Gaussian, has zeros on this phase space, and the zeros of finitely squeezed GKP states move with the widths of the peaks and of the envelope, and reach this phase space for fine-tuned widths. These features echo the role of the vacuum as the resource that makes GKP-based architectures hard to simulate.

Abstract: We formulate the standard quantum teleportation protocol within the framework of deformation quantization, employing the Stratonovich--Weyl (SW) correspondence and the associated star product on the coadjoint orbits of $SU(2)$. Each qubit is represented by a spherical phase space $S^2$ endowed with the Kirillov--Kostant--Souriau (KKS) symplectic structure. Within this setting, we explicitly construct the phase space representations of the entangled Bell resource, the controlled-NOT and Hadamard operators, the projective measurements, and the conditional Pauli corrections. Upon consideration of a conditional unitary transformation, the measurement outcome produces a Wigner function on the receiver's coadjoint orbit that is related to the input by an outcome-dependent rotation, which can subsequently be inverted through the implementation of an appropriate Pauli correction. The covariance of the SW correspondence provides a geometric interpretation of this process, in which quantum information is redistributed and reconstructed without transferring the underlying symplectic geometry. Our formulation can also be extended to study quantum teleportation in more general phase space geometries equipped with suitable star products.

Abstract: With D. Aerts, we showed in 2014, within the extended Bloch representation of quantum mechanics, that the Born rule can be understood as resulting from a universal average over all the possible ways in which the measurement-interactions can be selected. The cellular construction used there was since questioned, in the context of Bertrand's paradox, by N. Shackel, who observed that the result depends on the discretization adopted, that the sequence of averages is constant, and that the sense in which the average is taken over "all" densities remains unclear. Taking these criticisms as a stimulus, we re-examine the notion. The key is to recognize that a universal measurement is a random way of selecting a measurement which does not privilege any portion of the structure describing the available interactions, a property we call indifference. We prove that every indifferent universal measurement, however constructed, produces exactly the Born probabilities: the universal average is unique, and the cellular construction of 2014 is one among infinitely many ways of obtaining it, exact at every finite resolution. Non-trivial indifferent universal measurements exist, but must involve deterministic measurements: Dirichlet processes are indifferent universal measurements reaching every way of selecting an interaction, whereas an indifferent universal measurement free of deterministic components necessarily selects the uniform density, so that the Born rule holds not only on average but with certainty. Deviations from the quantum statistics in sequences of measurements are governed by a single parameter, in principle accessible to experiment. The only geometric inputs are the Euclidean length of the membrane and the orthogonal landing of the point-particle on it; the latter is where the content of Gleason's theorem lies. Generalizations to N outcomes and to Bertrand's paradox are discussed.

</details>

Abstract: A unified Fourier framework for quantum weight enumerators of prime-power-dimensional qudits is developed in this paper. We introduce the notions of Weyl power spectrum and Weyl-conjugation autocorrelation for the Weyl-operator quantities nderlying the Shor--Laflamme weight enumerators. Representing these quantities as formal vectors, we show that they form a symplectic Fourier transform pair, from which the quantum MacWilliams identity follows by applying the Hamming-weight functional. This framework extends to a family of twisted Fourier transforms, including the Rains shadow transform. We show that identical-copy Bell sampling corresponds to a twisted autocorrelation related to the shadow enumerator through Weyl translation and Hamming projection. We further prove that all twisted Fourier transforms are Weyl translations of either the ordinary MacWilliams transform or the Rains shadow transform. This two-class reduction implies that the twists yield no additional independent coefficientwise positivity constraints under compatible projections. Nevertheless, the framework leads to refined MacWilliams and shadow identities that retain more Fourier information than ordinary Hamming weight enumerators. We employ these refined identities in linear programming, together with simplex mapping and shadow-coset counting, to exclude surviving candidate weight distributions. These techniques yield tighter bounds for binary and qutrit quantum codes, including nonexistence results for specific code parameters.

Abstract: The Pusey--Barrett--Rudolph (PBR) theorem is commonly taken to establish that a pure quantum state represents a physical property of the system to which it is assigned, rather than merely encoding knowledge. In this work, I distinguish between state-first and dynamics-first physical theories, and argue that this reading presupposes the former. These two types of theories support different approaches to causal explanation, and one of PBR's stated assumptions depends on the state-first approach. Using a recent interpretation of quantum theory as a concrete example, I show how a dynamics-first theory can explain the PBR correlations while maintaining a broadly epistemic reading of quantum states. I conclude that the PBR theorem supports a dilemma: either the quantum state is part of reality, or reality is not fundamentally built out of states.

Abstract: Braiding Majorana zero modes does not by itself provide a universal set of quantum gates. Additional operations can be supplied through ancillary states, whose preparation need not have the same protection as braiding. Here, we consider purification of the non-Gaussian ancilla used for an entangling gate in Bravyi's scheme. We keep his elementary braid-and-fusion comparison and change how its outputs are combined. An additional layer compares the product of the three stabilizer signs. The first accepted errors giving an incorrect output then require three faulty input blocks rather than two. As a result, the preparation error decreases cubically instead of quadratically, and the asymptotic raw-copy overhead is reduced. Finally, we examine imperfect purification operations in an effective syndrome-noise model. The additional layer leaves the leading operation-error coefficient unchanged, although a nonzero error floor remains.

Abstract: Generative learning of a quantum measurement distribution from finite data requires choosing a model capacity that balances representation accuracy against estimation cost. We develop a bias-variance framework that decomposes the expected population Kullback-Leibler error at model capacity $s$ and sample size $N$ as $\mathcal E(s,N)=B(s)+V(s,N)$, with representation bias $B$ and a variance term $V$ capturing the mean estimation error. In the well-sampled regime, $V(s,N)\simeq[NC(s)]^{-1}$, and exponential or algebraic laws for $B$ and $C$ yield four scaling templates for the optimal error, from inverse powers of $N$ to inverse powers of $\ln N$. For local autoregressive models, the context radius sets the model capacity, and $B$ is the summed conditional mutual information lost by truncating the context. For a smoothed histogram learner, we derive an exact finite-sample estimation cost that accounts for rarely observed contexts and recovers the parameter-count law in the well-sampled regime. For transverse-field Ising Born distributions, the independently evaluated bias and estimation cost predict both the optimal context radius and the optimal error. In the well-sampled regime, the estimation cost grows exponentially with context radius. Its competition with approximately exponential bias decay in the gapped regimes predicts power-law error decay in $N$, while algebraic bias decay at criticality predicts asymptotic inverse-power decay in $\ln N$. Bulk-site results are consistent with the corresponding finite-sample predictions. These results connect the structure of quantum measurement distributions to the model capacity justified by finite data.

</details>

Abstract: This review aims to provide researchers, experimentalists, and quantum engineers with an updated and technically grounded perspective on the implementation of quantum gates in superconducting NISQ devices. The goal is to establish a comprehensive bridge between theoretical quantum circuit synthesis and its practical, experimental implementation, focusing on superconducting quantum technologies. Abstract: Quantum open-source software provides essential infrastructure for quantum science, and artificial intelligence (AI) is reshaping its capabilities and transforming its development. However, verifying quantum software at scale remains difficult, and AI-generated contributions can increase the workload of maintainers. In this Perspective, we examine verification as a central requirement for reliable AI-assisted quantum software development and research. We analyze public repositories, records covering 110 repositories from five annual quantum open-source hackathons that we organize, and responses from more than 1600 participants in a community survey. Our analysis connects limitations in automated verification with the review burden on quantum software maintainers. We propose verification practices and contribution standards that require contributors to support their changes with reproducible evidence for maintainer review. We also examine how quantum open-source software can provide infrastructure for AI-driven research whose results the community can independently verify and build upon. We maintain an open-source companion repository to support reproducibility and help projects adopt and refine these proposals. Together, these contributions help the quantum open-source software community translate advances in AI into reliable quantum research through reproducible verification.

</details>

Abstract: The positive-P representation for bosons allows scalable exact simulations of many-body open quantum systems, provided dissipation is strong enough to stabilise stochastic trajectories. Abstract: Random unitaries scramble quantum information into highly nonlocal degrees of freedom, thereby providing protection from local noise. However, as demonstrated by Turkeshi and Sierant (Phys. Rev. Lett. 132, 140401, 2024 and arXiv:2603.00235), this protection may fail once local noise grows sufficiently strong. These works describe a phase transition in a noisy random encoding-decoding circuit model between an error-protecting phase, in which encoded information is recoverable, and an error-vulnerable phase, in which encoded information is lost, with the transition occurring at a critical coherent noise angle $α_c \approx 1.14$. Here, we present the first experimental observation of this transition, conducted on IBM's ibm_pittsburgh Heron r3 device for system sizes of up to $N = 26$ qubits. We detect this transition via three order parameters: fidelity, Rényi-2 entanglement entropy, and stabilizer Rényi-2 entropy. For the latter two, we employ randomized measurement protocols to construct estimators, and for all three, we rely on zero-noise extrapolation and M3 SPAM error mitigation. We then jointly model the finite-size scaling collapse across all three order parameters with a Gaussian process regression, yielding a critical angle $α_c \approx 1.01$, well below the theoretical value. Inputting noiseless simulated data into the same model yields $α_c \approx 1.05$, implying that the discrepancy between experiment and theory is due to our encoding circuits, which are not deep enough to be truly Haar-random. Finally, we observe that the same model cannot adequately identify critical behavior in unmitigated, raw experimental data. This indicates that error mitigation techniques are crucial for detecting the transition on hardware.

Abstract: Passive loss removes probability from a system and therefore seems unable to improve an unconditional target. This intuition is exact for classical population dynamics under sink-only removal: passive killing obeys a finite-strength monotonicity principle even after optimization over the remaining controls. Here we show that coherent quantum dynamics can violate this classical monotonicity because coherent contributions to the final target can interfere destructively. We derive a trajectory-level criterion that determines when such a detrimental contribution is physically accessible to passive loss and accumulates into a net unconditional benefit. The criterion is encoded in a response operator constructed entirely from the lossless dynamics: its spectrum determines whether weak passive loss can help, while its principal eigenvector identifies the optimal environmental mode. Applying this criterion to vibronically assisted transfer, we identify a beneficial mode formed by coherently combining two individually detrimental local loss directions. The predicted mode remains near-optimal at finite loss, producing an \(8.73\%\) enhancement over the independently optimized lossless benchmark, compared with \(9.52\%\) under full finite-loss optimization. Our results establish how coherent quantum dynamics can evade classical passive-loss monotonicity and provide a general task-dependent principle for designing beneficial passive environments from lossless dynamics.

Abstract: In fault-tolerant Clifford+T circuits, a Boolean oracle costs T gates and extra qubits, and on four or more qubits every exact Toffoli gate needs an extra qubit. Gosset, Kothari and Zhang showed that sampling a fresh random circuit for each call makes the T-count of the Toffoli gate independent of its size, but a direct implementation of their sampler uses about one extra qubit per bit of precision. We show that computing the sampled parities in place, on the gate's own controls, removes every extra qubit once the gate is large enough: each random circuit then uses about 8 T gates per bit of precision and borrows only the gate's own idle inputs. This is one T gate more per circuit than the best measurement-free variant that keeps one clean ancilla. Abstract: A coherent sum of quantum circuits introduces interference that can alter the training signal. We distinguish a single linear combination of unitaries (LCU) from a circuit that repeatedly alternates branch operations and ancilla mixing. For a single-pass LCU, independent branches with zero matrix means cannot erase the differentiated branch's gradient variance: the total variance is at least the branch variance times the fourth power of its coefficient magnitude. This bound holds at any gate position and does not require the circuit unitaries to be Haar-random. Beyond this lower bound, we derive exact loss and gradient formulas that quantify the additional contributions from interference between branches. Matchgate examples exhibit polynomial single-pass LCU variances and exponential concentration under joint Haar-random sampling. Abstract: In multiparameter quantum estimation, incompatible measurements can impose trade-offs among estimation errors. Understanding the precision attainable with a finite number of jointly measured copies is therefore important, but direct optimization becomes costly as the tensor product space grows exponentially. For independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) copies, the quantum statistical model is invariant under permutations of the copies. We exploit this symmetry through Schur--Weyl duality to remove permutation multiplicity spaces while using their dimensions as weights. This gives a reduced statistical model equivalent to the original under arbitrary collective measurements. To approximate the tight bound, we use an exact quadratic identity to correct finite-ray dual solutions and obtain rigorous upper and lower bounds. For fixed copy number and parameter dimension, sufficiently fine meshes admit feasible primal points, and dual solutions with bounded objective gaps have uniformly bounded compact-search radii. These results yield quadratic convergence in the projective covering radius. Numerical examples illustrate the quadratic scaling of the mesh correction term and the computational reach of the reduced NH formulation.

Abstract: Magic, a quantum resource distinct from entanglement, measures departure from states stabilized by products of Pauli operators. Such stabilizer states can be maximally entangled yet admit efficient classical description: quantum advantage requires magic. We develop a powerful framework to evaluate the degree of magic in generic many-body states. We use it to study the fate of magic under thermal and dynamical evolution. In the transverse field Ising model, we find a thermal sudden death of magic that saturates with system size, and occurs after the death of entanglement. We reach similar conclusions in generic spin chains, and in the Kitaev quantum spin liquid on the honeycomb lattice, where the threshold shows an intricate dependence on subregion shape. We then study how magic equilibrates after a quantum quench. In quenches of generic spin chains, the magic of a subregion typically dies before entanglement, contrary to thermal trajectories. An explanation is given in terms of the geometry of non-magical states. As a third type of trajectory, we examine the fate of magic with separation with an appropriate notion of non-local magic and establish a sudden death. Finally, we show how to experimentally witness a volume law of frame-robust magic in quantum materials by using neutron scattering.

Abstract: The quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) is a paradigmatic variational quantum algorithm for combinatorial optimization problems, yet on current quantum devices the available qubit count and achievable circuit depth remain the main bottlenecks to its practical application. Divide-and-merge reduces the number of variables encoded in a single quantum circuit, while contraction based on coarse graining extends the effective range of shallow circuits, but existing work has not addressed both challenges simultaneously. We propose symmetry-preserving hierarchical QAOA (SP-HQAOA), in which parity sector contraction preserves the zero-field Ising structure of the effective objective at every contraction step, thereby addressing both within a single algorithm. We further prove that this reduction is the unique one with this property. Successive contractions further decouple the maximum leaf block size $B$ from the qubit limit $Q$ per quantum call, so every quantum circuit uses at most $Q$ qubits even when $B>Q$. We also establish a deterministic repair-risk bound on the possible loss in optimal value caused by each sector restriction, and the resulting bounds can be summed across contraction steps and levels of the recursive hierarchy. With depth $p=1$, SP-HQAOA attains $99.4\%$ to $99.7\%$ of the exact optimum on $N=40$ random regular MaxCut leaf blocks and, with at most $10$ qubits per circuit, $96\%$ of the classical reference for $N=500$. SP-HQAOA demonstrates the capacity to use shallow quantum circuits to address combinatorial optimization problems whose variable count far exceeds the qubit limit of a single quantum call, and may enable practical quantum optimization advantages in the near term.

Abstract: Quantum singular value transformation (QSVT) provides a unifying framework for quantum algorithms by applying the polynomials of quantum signal processing (QSP) to block-encoded matrices. We connect this framework with spectral analysis, design and topological protection in condensed matter physics. Second, a spectral mapping theorem determines the full spectrum of the QSVT operator, including continuous spectra, from the embedded operator through the QSP polynomial, allowing spectral analysis without translation invariance. Third, the spectral mapping turns QSP into spectral design: the QSP phases can stretch, fold and separate the bands and place the eigenvalues. Finally, the kernel sectors that conventional QSVT usually sets aside carry a topological index, invariant under the choice of QSP phases, local perturbations and gap-preserving deformations, which guarantees topologically protected states. These results unify two quantum walks: Szegedy's walk from quantum algorithms and the split-step walk from condensed matter physics. Abstract: Grover-Mixer QAOA (GM-QAOA) is a promising quantum framework for constrained combinatorial optimization. However, its practical implementation requires the efficient preparation of a uniform superposition over feasible states. In this work, we propose a two-stage variational approach. First, a parameterized quantum circuit is trained to prepare this superposition, using an entropy-based cost function that favors a uniform distribution over feasible bitstrings. The trained circuit is then embedded in GM-QAOA, where it provides both the initial state and, together with its inverse, yields the Grover mixer. The resulting method is evaluated on two representative benchmark problems: the Maximum Weight Independent Set (MWIS) problem and the Multidimensional Knapsack Problem (MKP). While the results demonstrate consistent improvements over standard QAOA for MWIS, only marginal gains are observed for MKP, with the advantage diminishing as the problem size increases. The reduced performance on MKP reflects the growing difficulty of training the variational mixer, likely due to increasingly challenging optimization landscapes. Overall, these findings highlight both the potential of variational Grover mixers and the trade-off between their performance gains and the additional resources required for their training.

Abstract: Topological Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes in more than two spatial dimensions offer features useful for fault-tolerant quantum computation, such as transversal non-Clifford gates and single-shot decoding, but a systematic understanding of them is still lacking. One approach is to decouple a code: map it to a stack of higher-form toric codes by a finite-depth local CSS-preserving Clifford circuit, possibly after adding ancillas. For translation-invariant $p$-ary CSS codes, where $p$ is prime ($p=2$ for qubits), we require the circuit to commute with the translations of a superlattice. In two dimensions, such a decoupling exists once these translations act trivially on every anyon type. In higher dimensions, excitations are sorted by their dimension into superselection layers, which can remain glued together even when translations act trivially on each layer. We show that this gluing is the only remaining obstruction: a code can be decoupled on a superlattice exactly when, after coarse-graining to it, translations act trivially on every layer and the gluing vanishes. Moreover, for a code with finitely many excitation types in each layer and $m$ nonzero layers, once translations act trivially on every layer, further coarse-graining by a factor of $p^{m-1}$ in every direction removes the aforementioned gluing. Physically, the same criterion determines when the code encodes the maximal number of logical qudits on a finite torus. Finally, we give an explicit algorithm that constructs the decoupling as a circuit of SUM (qudit CNOT) and SWAP gates.

</details>

These signatures provide complementary spectral tests of the staggered WS ladder and a calibration-aware route to precision force sensing.

Abstract: Quenched thermodynamics combines the equilibrium physics of fixed coupling configurations with a prescribed distribution of disorder. Problems related to quenched thermodynamics are generally intractable to approximate. Abstract: Exactly solvable models provide opportunities to understand complex optimization problems while offering controlled benchmarks for computational methods. However, nontrivial models that combine analytical tractability with challenging optimization structure remain scarce. Here, we introduce the Generalized Mean-Field Ising (GMFI) family, a densely connected and inhomogeneous class of Ising Hamiltonians whose ground states can nevertheless be obtained exactly. This exact solution reveals a hidden low-dimensional structure and enables a complete number-theoretic classification of different system sizes and degenerate ground states. Uniquely, the GMFI family can be used to benchmark both quantum and classical optimization methods over system sizes far beyond exact diagonalization. These results establish GMFI models as a rare bridge between exact many-body theory, number theory, optimization, and quantum hardware, providing a scalable benchmark for identifying and understanding algorithmic limitations in classical and quantum computing.

Abstract: Atomic clocks achieve extraordinary precision through quantum control over large ensembles of atoms, with precision increasing and atom number advancing steadily. Emerging nuclear clock technology encodes clock states in nuclear rather than electronic transitions, offering a new frontier for precision measurement. Here we study whether a large ensemble of such clock nuclei could be used to observe their mutual gravitational interaction. We investigate the gravitational redshift induced by neighboring atoms, including nuclear energy superpositions as sources of gravity, giving rise to a genuinely quantum gravitational interaction between pairs of nuclei. We find that the resulting clock frequency shift due to mutual gravity may become non-negligible for a macroscopic number of nuclei, for instance in a solid-state thorium clock, and we propose approaches to mitigate electromagnetic field effects by exploiting spatial symmetry and high-precision spectroscopy. We further discuss how energy-induced mass superpositions could be directly probed using external gravitational field sensors. By contrast, observing gravity-induced entanglement involving clocks would require substantially greater experimental advances due to decoherence from spontaneous emission. Taken together, our results suggest that nuclear clocks, either alone or in combination with complementary sensors such as optomechanical systems, may offer a future route toward probing gravity at short distances and testing gravitating quantum source masses beyond the Newtonian limit.

In particular, the 4D Toric code admits a fixed, constant-depth syndrome-based correction cycle without a multiscale decoder or expansion-based encoding. We do not claim constant total-depth overhead.
  3. Ideal decoding under local-stochastic noise. Abstract: Can measurements chosen adaptively, in response to earlier outcomes, be exponentially more informative than measurements fixed in advance? In many quantum settings, adaptivity helps at most polynomially; known exponential advantages require coherent measurements across copies or restricted measurements. We show an exponential advantage measuring one copy at a time, with no quantum memory kept between state copies, even when the nonadaptive learner may perform arbitrary global measurements on each copy. We construct explicit families of multi-qubit states in which a scalar parameter is encoded in one of exponentially many candidate observables, with an auxiliary marker indicating which one. An adaptive protocol identifies the relevant observable and then measures it using only single-qubit measurements and one round of adaptation. At fixed accuracy, it needs logarithmically many copies as the qubit count grows, whereas every nonadaptive protocol needs exponentially many. The same mechanism yields an exponential gap in local asymptotic minimax mean squared error constants, tight up to the exponential base. The states are separable and classical in label-dependent product bases; no entanglement or other nonclassical correlations are required. The gap comes from measurement incompatibility: the candidate observables cannot be measured jointly, so a fixed measurement must divide its information budget among them, while adaptivity selects the informative one. Accordingly, the separation disappears when the candidate observables commute, and cannot occur with fully observed classical samples. Measurement incompatibility thus emerges as a resource for quantum learning, distinct from entanglement and quantum memory, that classical feedback alone can exploit.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum experiments without a definite causal order offer new ways to distinguish devices, estimate physical parameters, and communicate. We prove a common limit on these tasks: the geometric Rényi divergence at the output of a deterministic completely positive process cannot exceed the sum of the divergences of its individual channel uses. The inequality holds for every Rényi order in $(0,1)\cup(1,2]$ and any number of independently replaceable channels. Parallel experiments with separate reference-assisted inputs approach the bound, giving an exact equality after optimization over processes. We also prove an additive bound on the right logarithmic derivative Fisher information of parameter-dependent channels under every fixed process in this class. A resulting Cramér--Rao bound limits locally unbiased estimation precision without assuming a definite causal order, and an erasure-probability example attains the bound. We show how this requirement controls positive local operations, and how small channel perturbations convert the divergence inequality into a Fisher-information bound, including for singular channels. A Rydberg atom array embedding is therefore not merely a hardware mapping of an optimization problem, but a co-designed architecture for quantum optimization in which embedding, computation, and error correction emerge from the same physical structure.

Abstract: A tournament solution records a set of winners from a tournament, that is, from the outcomes of all pairwise comparisons among $N$ alternatives. We study the quantum query complexity of computing tournament solutions when the outcome of each comparison has to be learned by a query. Classically, the picture is uniform: the Copeland set, the uncovered set and the top cycle all require $Θ(N^2)$ queries, and so does finding a single vertex of maximum out-degree. We show that the quantum query complexity of the top cycle can be computed with $\tildeΘ(N)$ queries, and a Condorcet winner can be found with $\tilde Θ(\sqrt N)$ queries. Finding a strong king requires $Θ(N^{3/2})$ queries, the same as finding a vertex of maximum out-degree. We also give a self-contained alternative proof of the known $Θ(N^{3/2})$ bound for finding a vertex of maximum degree, established by Gilani, Mande, Patro, and Raja. Our lower bounds reduce counting problems to tournament problems using Ambainis's adversary method. For the top cycle, we find a Hamiltonian path and locate the prefix forming the top cycle by divide and conquer.

Abstract: Quantum key distribution (QKD) offers information-theoretic security, but device imperfections introduce implementation vulnerabilities. Abstract: Bayes' rule is central to classical statistical inference: given a prior and a stochastic model, it determines the corresponding retrodictive conditional probabilities. In quantum theory, however, the question of whether an equally general rule of retrodiction exists and, if so, whether it is uniquely determined remains nontrivial. Here we prove that a retrodiction assignment defined for all priors and channels, which takes each predicted output state back to its prior, must coincide with the Petz transpose map if it is involutive, respects sequential and parallel composition, and agrees with unitary inversion. The theorem holds for channels between finite-dimensional full matrix algebras, with the input and output spaces restricted to the supports of the corresponding reference states. In particular, affinity under probabilistic mixtures and the characteristic square-root dependence on the reference states follow from these requirements rather than being postulated. Without involutivity, the remaining requirements characterize a family of doubly rotated Petz maps. Our result thus explains why the Petz transpose map arises in seemingly different areas of quantum theory: it is the unique quantum analogue of Bayes' rule compatible with these consistency requirements.

The resulting $k$-hop construction has causal radius at most $(k+1)p$ at depth $p$; for the $k=1$ architectures studied numerically, this gives radius $2p$. Across cycles, circular ladders, and high-girth regular bipartite graphs, $X{+}XX$ and $X{+}YY$ improve the best-found approximation ratios over the standard $X$ mixer at equal depth, while $X{+}YY$ at depth $p$ comes within about one approximation-ratio point of plain $X$ at depth $2p$ in the reach-matched cases examined. The unequal performance of the equal-reach $XX$ and $YY$ augmentations shows that satisfying a structural capability does not by itself guarantee performance. Together, the results provide complementary structural insights for mixer design.

Abstract: After truncating the matter Hilbert space of a single-mode light-matter model, gauge transformations leave the spectrum invariant whereas the ground-state photon number is gauge dependent, hence, its meaning remains disputed. Namely, the ground-state photon number is a quadratic polynomial in the gauge parameter $λ$, $\langle a^{\dagger} a\rangle_λ$ $=$ $\langle a^{\dagger} a\rangle_{0}$ $+$ $Cλ$ $+$ $Qλ^2$. Here the coefficients are the correlator $C=\langle G\,p\rangle$ of $G$ and the field momentum $p$, and $Q=\langle G^2\rangle$. Its minimum over all gauges is naturally gauge-invariant, and for the quantum Rabi model it is determined by the ground-state energy alone. We show that it is strictly positive for the quantum Rabi and Dicke models with level splitting, so that a residual photon content survives in every gauge. At weak coupling the gauge of minimal photon number is the Jaynes-Cummings gauge, and for the Dicke model it remains so at all couplings in the thermodynamic limit.

Abstract: Quantum reference frame (QRF) transformations relate the description of a physical system to different quantum observers, but their connection to concrete experimental phenomena remains largely unclear. Here we show that the trajectory-sharpening effect obtained by correlating a probed system with an effective negative-mass reference admits a natural interpretation as a QRF transformation to the frame of a negative-mass system. Using a minimal toy model of free particles with signed masses, we identify the Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen-type observables responsible for this effect with a pair of commuting relational observables that, under the instantaneous QRF transformation, are mapped to operators local to distinct subsystems. We then show that the corresponding relational trajectory is reproduced by the dynamical QRF transformation, where the transformed relational position acquires an explicit time dependence. We introduce QRF-invariant sharpness measures for relational trajectories and derive monogamy relations showing that a trajectory cannot be made simultaneously arbitrarily sharp with respect to two distinct negative-mass frames. We further show that trajectory sharpness can instead reside in a collective reference mode encoded across two physical systems, even when neither constituent alone provides an arbitrarily sharp reference, so that sharpness can be distributed rather than localized in a single constituent frame. These results provide an operational, experimentally relevant interpretation of QRF transformations for continuous-variable systems.

</details>

Abstract: QAOA mixers play an important role in enabling exploration of the exponentially large solution space of binary optimization problems. In this work, inspired by quantum-walk mixing theory, we propose two complementary spectral heuristics for characterizing QAOA mixer topologies. The first is the normalized minimum eigenvalue separation of the mixer Hamiltonian $H_M$, while the second is the long-time averaged minimum eigenvalue separation of the mixer unitary $U_M(β)=e^{-iβH_M}$. Both quantities are invariant under any positive rescaling of the mixer Hamiltonian and provide Hamiltonian- and unitary-level characterizations of the mixer's spectral structure. We use them as heuristic indicators of the mixer dynamics relevant to amplitude redistribution during QAOA evolution.

We then compare these separations with empirical QAOA performance. For random permutation-invariant cost Hamiltonians, the observed performance ordering is broadly consistent with the spectral characterization. In contrast, for structured 1-D and 2-D Ising QUBOs, this ordering breaks down, indicating that mixer-cost alignment can become important. These results illustrate both the usefulness and the limitations of topology-level, cost-agnostic spectral heuristics for QAOA mixer selection.

Abstract: Quantum error correction is essential for fault-tolerant quantum computing. High-rate quantum low-density parity-check (qLDPC) codes have emerged as a promising approach to reducing the substantial physical qubit overhead of quantum error correction, enabling encoding rates exceeding $1/2$ in blocks of approximately one thousand physical data qubits. However, their practical implementation requires decoders that combine high accuracy with low latency to keep pace with quantum hardware. Existing decoders of high-rate qLDPC codes can achieve high accuracy but often incur substantial computational latency, whereas neural network decoders have yet to be demonstrated for high-rate qLDPC codes. Here, we introduce \emph{neural triage decoding}, a framework that uses syndrome consistency to adaptively allocate neural computation. A local neural message-passing network rapidly predicts error corrections, additional iterations reduce residual syndrome violations, and a rarely invoked diversification stage searches for consistent corrections to the remaining shots. We demonstrate that the \texttt{Triage} decoder can decode ultra-high-rate qLDPC codes encoding a few hundred logical qubits, including lifted-product and Kasai codes, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy comparable to the near optimal algorithmic decoders while reducing decoding latency by approximately one to two orders of magnitude. Our framework is inspired by a rigorous analysis of the exact bitwise marginal decoder, revealing a distance-independent syndrome-inconsistent error floor. In addition, we show the robustness of neural network decoders to mismatched error priors. Our results establish adaptive neural decoding as a promising route towards fast, accurate, and scalable quantum error correction in the near term.

</details>

Abstract: We consider the distribution of domain walls arising in ground states of frustrated 1-dimensional Ising spin chains with anti-parallel boundary spins. For ideal chains, a sine-squared particle-in-a-box distribution of domain walls is expected in the quantum setting, and a uniform distribution expected classically. However, for chains with random imperfections, prior work [Chancellor et al., npj Quantum Inf. 8, 73, 2022] found that the distribution in a physical implementation corresponded to neither. Instead, the domain wall distribution forms a U-shaped profile arising from a classical model with random fields, where the combination of averaging over these fields and thermal averaging produces the characteristic U-shape. With D-Wave's newer fast anneal feature, we access anneal times shorter than the coherence time and observe the expected coherent quantum behavior in the form of a sine-squared distribution. For the shortest available anneal time, the dynamics have not yet reached the sine-squared shape and instead display a different U-shape in the middle of the chains. This motivates using the extent of the domain wall distribution tail in this regime as a platform independent metric that provides a signature of both coherence time and noise levels.

This operation increases the radius of the initial measurement contexts of a perfect quantum strategy for the game, while preserving classical unsatisfiability. We then apply parallel repetition to make the classical value small, and we turn the system into a pseudo-telepathic multi-layered Kneser hypergraph via a PCP construction of Dinur, Guruswami, Khot, and Regev [STOC'03].
