Abstract: Left-handed (LH) superconducting transmission lines, built with series capacitors and shunt inductors instead of the series inductors and shunt capacitors of a conventional right-handed (RH) line, have a spectrum with a nonzero lower band edge and modes that become sparse at high frequencies. We study the dynamical Casimir effect in a LH line terminated by a flux-modulated SQUID and compare it with an RH line built from the same elements and with a long coplanar waveguide terminated by the same SQUID. We derive the quantum Hamiltonian and show that the coupling between two modes factorizes into their amplitudes at the SQUID. Together, the LH spectrum and the mode dependence of this coupling determine which modes are populated and how fast. With equal elements, the LH coupling grows with frequency and exceeds the RH one above a crossover pump frequency set by the cell inductance and capacitance, and only the LH line has resonances above the RH band edge. Pumping two modes at once lets a weakly coupled mode borrow coupling from a strongly coupled one, while crossings of resonance lines populate three or four modes without increasing the growth rate. Compared with the coplanar waveguide, the LH line has a similar coupling but keeps the created particles in the target mode, whereas the nearly equidistant waveguide spreads them over many pairs and needs more pump energy.

Abstract: Nonlocal correlation functions underpin lattice calculations of parton physics. However, their extraction is limited by a rapidly deteriorating signal-to-noise ratio with increasing Euclidean separation and hadron momentum, and by excited-state contamination. Abstract: Spin space groups provide a complete symmetry classification of magnetic structures in the non-relativistic limit and have become an increasingly useful language for organizing magnetic phases and their physical properties. Each spin space group with a given parent space group is specified, up to spin-only symmetries, by a real representation of that space group acting in spin space. We argue that working directly with this representation not only avoids the construction and identification of the group, but also organizes and simplifies the resulting symmetry constraints on allowed responses. Determining the presence or absence of magnetic compensation, piezomagnetism, spin and charge transport, the Edelstein effect, and momentum-space spin textures becomes an ordinary problem in the representation theory of space groups. The resulting conditions reduce to overlaps between the three exterior powers of the defining representation and multipole representations of the point group. Several physically distinct responses then reduce to the same condition on the defining representation. For example, a $p$-wave spin texture and the time-even Edelstein effect are allowed under the same condition, as are piezomagnetism and a $d$-wave spin texture in compensated order. The wave-vector content of the exterior powers gives a further hierarchy of selection rules: depending on the relevant power, a uniform response requires one, two, or three ordering wave vectors to sum to a reciprocal lattice vector. These rules can exclude many responses before point-group symmetry is considered. We illustrate the approach with examples drawn from a wide variety of materials hosting collinear, coplanar and non-coplanar magnetic order. Finally, we discuss extensions of these results to magnetic space groups, connections to Landau theories of collinear order, and the characterization of odd-parity magnets.

</details>

Abstract: The chiral central charge $c$ measures the gravitational anomaly of a two-dimensional topological phase. It fixes the heat current carried along the edge. So far, it has been accessed through thermal transport, where phonons complicate the signal, or through wave-function probes that need a pure ground state. Here we show that $c$ is a spectroscopic quantity of the edge. A weak modulation of the edge couplings that travel along the edge is absorbed according to the Virasoro algebra. Normalized by the response to a static change of the same couplings, the absorbed weights obey $D_n=2n\langle L_0\rangle+\frac{c}{12}(n^3-n)$, so that $\mathcal{C}\equiv2D_2-4D_1=c$ in any state of the ideal edge theory, thermal or not. The central charge thus appears in transition weights, although it cancels from excitation gaps. The ratio of line weights with and without a vison in the bulk, $Λ=4h_σ/c$, needs no calibration and fixes the conformal spin $h_σ$ of the non-Abelian Ising anyon. For the Kitaev honeycomb spin liquid we find $\mathcal{C}=0.49999$ and $h_σ=0.06250$ on 7168 spins. Calibrating with the shift of the measured line itself gives $c$ within about $1\%$ on 96 spins and within $1\%$ under $20\%$ random coupling errors. Abstract: Quantum entanglement is a fundamental resource that gives rise to correlations with no classical counterpart in strongly correlated fermionic systems. However, its characterization in mixed quantum states remains challenging. Established entanglement diagnostics, such as logarithmic negativity, are computationally demanding in interacting systems, and their evaluation by exact diagonalization is restricted to small system sizes. Meanwhile, conventional Rényi proxies, including twisted and untwisted Rényi negativities, can be positive even for separable states. In this work, we introduce a family of \textit{relative Rényi negativities} that serve as entanglement witnesses. By relating fermionic partial time reversal to the size superoperator, we establish a path-integral representation for these Rényi negativities, enabling their evaluation in thermal states of the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model and its generalizations via a large-$N$ expansion. Applying this method, we investigate entanglement in both the original SYK model and the Maldacena-Qi coupled SYK model. We identify a special class of relative Rényi negativities which serve as plausible proxies for logarithmic negativity and uncover first-order entanglement transitions that are invisible to simple observables. These results highlight the utility of relative Rényi negativities as probes of mixed-state entanglement in strongly correlated fermionic systems and suggest the existence of a new class of transitions where these negativities serve as free energies.

Abstract: Recent advances in microscopic many-body methods have enabled increasingly accurate descriptions of equilibrium quantum states, yet large-scale simulations of emergent-order dynamics typically rely on phenomenological equations with prescribed free-energy functionals. We introduce the Adiabatic Variational Principle (AVP), a framework for deriving order-parameter dynamics directly from an interacting many-body Hamiltonian. When microscopic relaxation is fast compared with order-parameter evolution, the many-body state follows the collective configuration adiabatically, in analogy with the Born--Oppenheimer separation of electronic and nuclear motion. This assumption makes explicit the adiabatic closure implicit in conventional phase-field descriptions. Each instantaneous order-parameter configuration then defines a constrained quasi-stationary many-body state, and the Lagrange multipliers enforcing these constraints directly provide the thermodynamic forces driving the collective evolution. Nonlinear Mori--Zwanzig projection theory further connects the reversible dynamics to the microscopic algebra of the collective variables and relates dissipation and fluctuations to the eliminated degrees of freedom. Applications to charge- and spin-density-wave dynamics recover Allen--Cahn domain growth, resolve evolving domain networks together with their microscopic electronic structure, and yield generalized Landau--Lifshitz dynamics for itinerant magnetism. The AVP thus provides a solver-independent microscopic foundation for phase-field-like simulations of the nonequilibrium dynamics of emergent electronic order.

Abstract: Within quantum many-body phase diagrams, continuous transition lines can appear and then terminate. However, it has remained unclear whether the neighboring regimes can be meaningfully described as distinct phases, akin to water and vapour, since any sharp characterization would have to go beyond the usual notion of phases of matter. We answer this in the affirmative by introducing quasi-phases: gapped regimes near a critical point that are connected in the broader phase diagram, but not by any gapped path accessible from small perturbations of the critical theory. The physical consequences include interface modes between regimes in the same phase of matter. More striking still, describing both the extended critical line and the gapped path connecting the regimes requires a bigger multicritical parent theory. In spin-chain examples with continuous terminating lines with central charge $c=1$, we find multicritical $c=3/2$ and $c=9/5$ orbifolds describing so-called 'quotient symmetry protected' and 'representation symmetry protected' topological phenomena. While one-dimensional symmetry-protected topological phases are labeled by projective representation classes, these quasi-phases can have interface modes carrying higher-dimensional linear representations. We turn this into a constructive principle through a weakening of the Lieb-Schultz-Mattis anomaly. Supporting distinct quasi-phases makes an unnecessary critical point infrared-necessary, even if it is unnecessary at the ultraviolet scale. This condition is more restrictive than simply having a codimension-one critical line, revealing richer structure within phases of matter than their conventional classification suggests.

Abstract: We develop an algebraic theory of fusion, mobility, and statistics in abelian gapped fracton orders and topological phases. The framework generalizes the algebraic description of abelian anyons to systems with restricted-mobility and spatially extended excitations. We consider translation-invariant bosonic systems whose pointlike and extended excitations have finite fusion order. The basic objects are braided fusion complexes (BFCs): chain complexes encoding fusion and mobility, equipped with a braiding chain map encoding statistics. Their equivalence classes define braided fusion theories (BFTs). Our framework encompasses arbitrary translation-invariant Pauli codes, as well as all abelian anyon theories in two spatial dimensions, including chiral theories. We give evidence for the conjecture that every nondegenerate BFT can be realized as the excitation theory of a gapped phase and that the BFT describing such a phase is a phase invariant. We derive constraints on pointlike fusion and mobility, showing, for example, that neither the Pauli X nor Z sectors of the X-cube and anisotropic models can occur alone in a nondegenerate BFT. For nondegenerate theories with only pointlike excitations, we extract a simpler theory of mutual statistics, consisting of a fusion module and a map to its statistical dual. This theory of mutual statistics is an invariant of the BFT and, at odd fusion order, gives an equivalent description of the full braided fusion theory. We interpret remote detection as the generalization of mutual statistics to fracton orders. In the BFC setting, we give a precise formulation of the excitation-detector principle: every nontrivial excitation can be detected by an operator supported at spatial infinity, and every nontrivial detector detects some excitation. Assuming a unique infinite-space ground state, we prove that this principle is equivalent to nondegeneracy.

Abstract: Stationary learning admits an ideal-gas description under restrictive noise assumptions. In neural networks whose gradient noise departs from these assumptions, however, an entropy-scaling relation resembling a Maxwell relation remains approximately valid. We ask which thermodynamic-looking relations follow from scale symmetry rather than equilibrium, and how departures from the ideal-gas description can be quantified, for discrete stochastic gradient dynamics with memory. An exact rescaling of trajectories explains the entropy relation without assuming thermal equilibrium. Within a local harmonic approximation, self-adjusting weight norms yield a spectral equation of state whose virial coefficients are set by moments of the curvature spectrum, and coupled parameter groups can sustain stationary probability currents. Our central result beyond this approximation is an exact curvature-transport identity. Together with a finite-step loss balance, it decomposes corrections to the harmonic fluctuation relation into measurable curvature changes, finite-step remainders, gradient-memory correlations and finite-window drift terms. A related displacement-weighted curvature change provides a diagnostic of the arrow of time; its mean and second moment give a lower bound on the relative entropy between forward and reversed state pairs. We also calculate the activation-boundary contribution to curvature for rectified teacher-student networks with Gaussian inputs. Tests in sphere models, teacher-student networks and convolutional networks on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 support the transport description and identify limits of the harmonic and cubic-remainder approximations. These results separate symmetry-based relations from equilibrium assumptions and provide quantitative diagnostics of nonideal fluctuations in learning dynamics.

Abstract: Rare events and large-deviation theory have attracted considerable interest in the study of random walks. Here, we investigate the rare events of the positional distribution of continuous-time random walks under ordinary and equilibrium initial conditions using the saddle point method. We further find that the most likely number of renewals required to reach a given position, as well as the associated rate functions, is insensitive to the initial conditions, whereas the far tails of the positional distribution retain a dependence on the initial conditions. Abstract: A major question in active matter is how interactions between particles are modified by activity. Here, we address this question for Coulomb interactions by considering a charged swimmer immersed in an electrolyte. A static charge is screened by an ionic cloud that decays over the Debye length $λ_D$. We show that the persistent motion of the swimmer can qualitatively modify the steady-state ionic charge-density profile. At low activity, the swimmer appears effectively quasistatic to the ions, and conventional Debye-Hückel screening is recovered. At higher activity, the ballistic-to-diffusive dynamics of the swimmer are reflected in distinct spatial regimes. A power-law decay is followed by a stretched-exponential decay at intermediate distances. At sufficiently large distances, exponential screening is restored, but with an increased screening length, $λ>λ_D$, revealing activity-induced underscreening. Thus, active motion modifies both the spatial form and the range of electrostatic screening. Our results motivate the study of active electrolytes composed of positively and negatively charged swimmers.

Abstract: The reliability and safety of artificial intelligence (AI), together with their transfer and evolution through knowledge distillation, have attracted growing attention. We establish an information-theoretic bound on the reliability--safety trade-off and characterize the dynamics of this trade-off under knowledge distillation. We first prove a universal trade-off inequality that is independent of the specific model form. Its boundary is determined solely by a single parameter Kh, which quantifies the hidden layer's ability to distinguish benign from hazardous inputs. We then develop a canonical Boltzmann-machine model of AI response behavior and derive two exact relations between reliability and safety. The trade-off curve is determined by the hazard discrimination capacity K, while the overall answer tendency controls the operating point along the curve. These relations agree well with existing experimental data. In knowledge distillation, we find that teacher responses induce a renormalization of the student model's trade-off parameters. We further use a renormalization-group approach to study multigenerational distillation, deriving exact recursion relations and the associated renormalization-group-like flows for two representative direct intergenerational distillation protocols at arbitrary teacher--student alignment strength. These flows reveal fixed-point structures, long-term dynamics, and critical behavior, yielding testable predictions for the critical transition between capability loss and stable transmission.

Abstract: The Gibbs states of local Hamiltonians have short-range conditional correlations. To maintain a steady state with long-range conditional correlations, one might expect that a stochastic process must ``pay'' either in nonlocal moves or entropy production. Surprisingly, we find strictly local Gaussian processes that obey detailed balance and have unique steady states with arbitrarily long-range conditional correlations. We show that such processes must still produce entropy, if implemented using a local quadratic Hamiltonian coupled locally to equilibrium, rapidly relaxing baths. We lower-bound this entropy production in terms of the steady state's conditional mutual information (CMI); if the CMI decays exponentially with distance, the associated length-scale is called the Markov length $ξ$. The entropy production per site per relaxation time obeys $\hatσ\ge c ξ^2$ for large $ξ$, and with $c$ an implementation-dependent constant. We also bound the tradeoff between coupling range and entropy production. We contrast these Gaussian processes with jump processes on discrete state spaces, for which, under certain irreducibility assumptions, steady states with long-range CMI are only possible if detailed balance is violated. We conjecture (and present evidence) that rapidly mixing local jump processes cannot stabilize steady states with long-range CMI.

</details>

Abstract: Rotary molecular motors commonly couple components with different rotational symmetries, but how this gearing controls energy transduction remains unclear. Here we show that symmetry can produce a fundamental tradeoff in coupled stochastic barrierless rotary motors: the gear ratio that maximizes output power under tight coupling is also the ratio that requires the strongest coupling to remain locked. Consequently, weakening the coupling can invert the effect of gearing, transforming a power maximum into a local minimum. At strong driving, both the power-optimal and most slip-susceptible ratios approach symmetry match. Numerical solutions of the Fokker-Planck dynamics for rotors with finite energy barriers reproduce these predictions and reveal how the behavior changes when driving strength is fixed per rotation or per subunit. Motivated by the bacterial flagellar motor, these results identify coupling strength as a key determinant of whether matched rotational symmetries enhance or suppress energy transduction, providing a general design principle for coupled molecular motors.

Abstract: We apply the Constructive Cavity Method (CCM) to the Ising perceptron. The process starts from positive soft channels admitting an auxiliary representation $$ U(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}} e^{tx}ρ(\mathrm{d}t),\qquad ρ\ge0, $$ with an auxiliary measure having a finite exponential quadratic moment. This rewrites the perceptron as a dense, bipartite Gaussian model with an Ising layer and a continuous auxiliary layer. The CCM blanket geometry then gives two constructive variance profiles, $Q_\ell$ on the Ising side and $Γ_\ell$ on the auxiliary side. The bipartite shell scale is $$ Q_{\ell+1}Γ_{\ell+1}-Q_\ellΓ_\ell, $$ and Gaussian core comparison removes the core contribution $ΔQ_\ellΔΓ_\ell$ under blanket refinement. The scalar cavity functional has three terms, $$ \mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{perc}}(ν)=\mathbb{E}\left[\log\langle2\cosh x\rangle_ν+α\log\langle U(s)\rangle_ν-\log\langle e^y\rangle_ν\right], $$ where $x$ is the new-spin field, $s$ is the new-constraint field, and $y$ is the cavity correction. CCM identifies the constructive cavity variables with the Aizenman--Sims--Starr random overlap structure (ROSt) variables. Parisi's recursive averaging then gives $$ \mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{perc}}^{\mathrm{CCM}}(ζ,Q,Γ)=X_0(0)+αY_0(0)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=0}^{R}ζ_\ell\left(Q_{\ell+1}Γ_{\ell+1}-Q_\ellΓ_\ell\right). $$ The replica-symmetric specialization gives Gardner's formula for the generalized Ising perceptron. Specializing the constraint terminal to the hard channel gives the Krauth--Mézard functional for the binary perceptron.

</details>

The lateral coupling, fixed by the coverage series, is the free energy of a neighbouring pair, with a vibrational pair entropy. The small deformation of the measured curves from the Chudley--Elliott form, the tilt, is proposed as an observable of the coupling. The tilts at $0.15$ and $0.2$~ML are consistent with it, although they are hard to extract from the published curves. The memory equation provides a transition-state theory for the adlayer, with the collective friction in the role of the friction of the substrate and without divergence at the crossover. Combined with the standard quantum transition-state theory of a single adsorbate, the extracted hop rates give the friction of the substrate, $124$ and $75$~ps$^{-1}$ for H and D, independent of the coverage.

Abstract: For Gaussian white noise the Itô and Stratonovich prescriptions differ by $O(dt)$ per time step, which accumulates into the familiar spurious drift $\tfrac12 D\,II'$. We show that for renewal shot noise with state-dependent gain, $\dot x=-C(x)-I(x)ξ[t]$ with $ξ[t]$ a train of Dirac spikes of finite random amplitude, the two prescriptions differ by a finite amount at every event, and that where $I$ vanishes the difference can become qualitative. The Marcus kick, the jump-process counterpart of the Stratonovich prescription, is the flow map of $dx/ds=I(x)$: no kick can cross a zero $x^\ast$ of $I$, for any jump and waiting-time statistics. The Itô kick is only the tangent map of that flow. For $I(x)=1+βx$ it crosses $x^\ast=-1/β$ if and only if $ξβ>1$ and collapses the whole line onto $x^\ast$ at $ξβ=1$; below threshold it is itself a Marcus kick with a remapped jump law. Bounded jump laws thus have a threshold, while unbounded ones leak across the barrier for \emph{any} parameters. The discrepancy cannot be absorbed into a renormalised drift beyond lowest order, and only the Marcus kick is generated by a single operator, which makes the master equation of Bianucci, Bologna and Mannella (2026) intrinsically Marcus. Numerically, the standard Heun kick is a second-order truncation of the Marcus map that preserves the barrier only because its order is even; it shortens every jump, biasing second moments in proportion to the squared jump amplitude and the jump kurtosis, and depleting the region near the barrier. We give the exact-flow integrator that replaces it.

</details>

Abstract: We study four paths that join the starting site to the final position of a simple random walk of \(N=L^2\) steps on the infinite cubic lattice. The paths are selected on the walk trace---the graph of visited sites and traversed bonds---by different rules: the undirected shortest path \(S_1\), the directed shortest path \(S_2\), the time-respecting shortest path \(S_3\), and the depth \(D_e\) of the final site in a depth-first-search tree. Monte Carlo simulations for \(L\) up to 32768 give mean lengths scaling as \(L^{d_O}\), with \(d_{S_1}=1.48005(9)\), \(d_{S_2}=1.49868(5)\), \(d_{S_3}=1.51205(5)\), and \(d_{D_e}=1.7099(7)\), so that \(d_{S_1}<d_{S_2}<d_{S_3}<d_{\rm LE}<d_{D_e}\), where $d_{\rm LE}=1.62400(5)$ is the known loop-erased-random-walk exponent. Imposing traversal directions increases the shortest-path exponent, and imposing time order increases it further; these constraints therefore change the scaling exponent, not only the amplitude. For every path, the standard deviation scales with the same exponent as the mean, so it is scale-invariant, unlike the visited-site mass; the rescaled length distributions collapse onto a finite-width scaling function. No analytical theory currently predicts these exponents; our results provide precise numerical benchmarks for future studies of path geometry on random-walk traces.

</details>

Abstract: The one-dimensional Ising chain with inverse-square interactions is a paradigmatic long-range system: it exhibits the unusual Thouless transition, with a discontinuity in the spontaneous magnetization, yet its full field-dependent thermodynamics remains unknown in closed form. We develop the high-temperature expansion of its free energy while retaining the dependence on a uniform magnetic field at every order. We prove that, after a natural rescaling of the coupling, each coefficient is a rational polynomial in the squared hyperbolic tangent of the reduced field. Remarkably, at each order $n$ the field-dependent free-energy coefficient of a finite, periodically repeated ring is an exact polynomial of degree at most $n$ in $L^{-2}$. The infinite-chain coefficient is its constant term and can therefore be reconstructed from $n+1$ finite rings without numerical extrapolation. Using this reconstruction, we determine the full field-dependent expansion through order 26: 26 polynomials containing 377 rational coefficient entries. We further derive exact formulas, valid at every order, for the coefficients of the two highest powers of the field variable using matching determinants and a cycle-Laplacian reduction. These results reveal an unexpected algebraic structure in the microscopic thermodynamics of the inverse-square chain and provide high-order information about its magnetic response.

Although the two variants are described by different Hamiltonians, they yield the same free energy. Moreover, both Hamiltonians lead to the same average energy and hence to the same microcanonical entropy. The canonical and microcanonical phase boundaries differ markedly, and so does the multicritical behaviour of the two ensembles. In the microcanonical ensemble, tricritical points appear for any fraction of active spins, whereas in the canonical ensemble they emerge only when this fraction is sufficiently large. At lower fractions, canonical critical endpoints arise instead. We also report the formation of gaps in the entropy as a function of magnetization, another manifestation of ensemble inequivalence.

Abstract: Beyond a critical point, two thermodynamic phases become continuously connected, without being separated by any thermodynamic singularity. The paradigmatic example is the liquid-gas transition, where several criteria have been proposed to distinguish liquid-like and gas-like regimes within the supercritical region. Recently, a pair of thermodynamic crossover lines, $L^\pm$, was introduced to address a proposed incompatibility of previous definitions with the universal critical behavior of the Ising model. These lines divide the supercritical region into three regimes, meet only at the critical point, and obey the expected critical scaling laws. Here, we demonstrate that these requirements do not uniquely determine the crossover boundaries. Instead, infinitely many pairs of lines satisfy the same conditions, with different choices of thermodynamic paths selecting different crossover boundaries. We further show that the Lee-Yang formalism, despite providing an intrinsic characterization of critical singularities, does not resolve this ambiguity in defining real-axis crossover boundaries. Restricting to regular probing paths that respect the $Z_2$ symmetry of the critical point recovers the Widom and $L^\pm$ prescriptions asymptotically. This restriction, however, is a choice of protocol rather than a property of the system, and even then two inequivalent prescriptions survive. Our results demonstrate that universal critical behavior alone does not select privileged supercritical thermodynamic crossover boundaries. While such lines may identify physically meaningful changes in thermodynamic, structural, or transport properties, their precise locations and the widths of the regions they delimit remain dependent on the operational criteria used to define them.

</details>

Abstract: Truncating weak interactions is a natural simplification of a many-body Hamiltonian, but the validity of this approximation need not be uniform in distance and time. In a chaotic system, a weak long-range coupling can seed a small disturbance far from its source, after which the local dynamics can amplify that disturbance exponentially. We study this mechanism in an infinite-temperature classical Heisenberg spin chain with a dominant nearest-neighbor exchange and a weak interaction tail. Using a classical decorrelator, we compare exponentially and power-law decaying tails over several decades in interaction strength. For exponentially decaying interactions, the long-distance front remains ballistic when the tail is sufficiently shallow, with a velocity controlled by the competition between the spatial decay of the seed and the local chaotic growth. For sufficiently rapid exponential decay, the nearest-neighbor front, which is defined by an intrinsic butterfly velocity $v_b$, is recovered within the accessible space-time window. For power-law interactions, the arrival time is logarithmic in distance for all exponents studied, corresponding to an exponentially expanding spatial front; the power-law exponent controls the coefficient of this logarithm. We interpret both behaviors using a seeding-and-amplification picture in which long-range couplings generate weak disturbances throughout the chain whose growth is governed primarily by the local nearest-neighbor dynamics. In this picture, the overall strength of the tail primarily controls when the long-range front becomes visible, while its spatial decay controls the propagation law. Thus a weak interaction tail can leave conventional spin-dynamical observables essentially unchanged while qualitatively altering the long-distance propagation of decorrelation.

</details>

Abstract: Local error correction can preserve classical information against noise, but protection under synchronized updates need not survive when sites evolve at independent random times. Establishing stability is particularly challenging when protection relies on the collective action of competing local rules. Here we develop a spacetime renormalization framework that reveals how independently acting local updates can collectively stabilize memory. We identify sufficient conditions under which the geometry of stable interfaces and suppression of defect propagation produce reliable erosion at large scales. Applying this framework, we prove stability of the asynchronous \textsf{R} squeezing code and introduce robust processes in two and three dimensions. In these systems, both encoded phases persist under sufficiently weak noise, even when the noise favors one phase. Our results provide a criterion for designing autonomous memories sustained by competing local stochastic dynamics.

Abstract: Artificial spin ices are geometric arrangement of nanomagnets that exhibit reconfigurable magnetization states and GHz dynamics. Their investigation in the GHz regime is challenging because of the existence of multiple modes and the impact of magnetization deviations at the edges of the nanomagnets. A minimal model to capture such dynamics require three macrospins per nanomagnet, an approach that was implemented for generic artificial spin ices in the tool Gaenice. Here, we use the Gaenice framework to realize a relaxation method to stabilize magnetization states self-consistently, called GaeniceN. We implement the dissipative component of the Landau-Lifshitz equation in an iterative approach to efficiently converge to the relaxed state. As an example, we find our approach to correctly predict the transition between phases in a pyramid ASI subject to a low-magnitude external field by computing the system's average energy. Because they are rooted on the same framework, GaeniceN produces the initial condition needed for Gaenice to compute dynamics such as ferromagnetic resonance or band structures. Combined, these tools will be able to aid in the design of artificial spin ices with applications in magnonics.

Abstract: In a ballistic system, the anomalous dc Hall response generically depends on microscopic details, including the geometric embedding of the orbitals and the precise implementation of the measurement. Abstract: Floquet phenomena - where a coherent, time-periodic drive hybridizes the conduction band with energy-replicated copies of the valence band - have been demonstrated with intense optical fields and, more recently, other bosonic excitations. However, within mean-field approximations and the current understanding of Floquet phenomena, these effects survive only for the coherence time of the drive, limiting practical use and current demonstrations to the sub-picosecond regime. Here, consistent with recent theoretical predictions beyond the mean-field picture, using time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy in WSe$_2$/MoS$_2$ heterostructures, we show that incoherent, dense populations of dark interlayer excitons hybridize the conduction band with energy-replicated copies of the valence band, mimicking Floquet-like phenomena - now persisting for nearly a nanosecond. With increasing interlayer exciton density, the valence band and its replica evolve from an inverted-parabolic to a Mexican-hat dispersion, a signature of the predicted non-zero static coupling that hybridizes the bands and scales linearly with the exciton density. By tuning twist angle, we further show that dark excitons with net momentum create replicas that hybridize with bands offset not just in energy but also in momentum, providing another powerful degree of freedom beyond conventional Floquet engineering. These results establish a robust, long-lived route to engineering electronic states, beyond a coherent, zero-momentum drive.

The extent to which moiré lattice reconstruction at different length scales affects the electronic structure through electro-mechanical coupling remains unresolved. Consequently, these heavy fermion states energetically coexist with itinerant $c$-electrons at the AB/BA sites. Abstract: Free fermions coupled to particle gain and loss relax to a Gaussian steady state, in which every natural orbital has its own occupation. We find the distribution of these occupations $\varrho_{\rm st}$ for a random Hamiltonian coupled to many random gain and loss channels. Each orbital is filled and emptied at its own rates, and the Hamiltonian, moving particles across the levels during their lifetime, averages the differences between the orbitals. We show that this averaging is incomplete: the spread of the occupations decreases only as the inverse square root of the number of channels per level. At large system size the distribution depends on the grain Hamiltonian only through its density of states $ν(E)$. The spread is bounded by the baths alone: even Hamiltonians with unbounded, heavy-tailed spectra cannot widen it beyond this bound. The spread suppresses the particle-number fluctuations below their binomial value.

Abstract: Large language model agents have begun to carry out laboratory measurements, but their scientific conclusions are useful only if they hold up under scrutiny. Demonstrating this requires controlled studies because models are stochastic and the context a model is given steers its conclusions. Here we introduce HERA, an autonomous agent designed to carry out cryogenic transport measurements of quantum devices. HERA draws on a knowledge base curated by lab members that holds measurement protocols, software tools, and literature references. We assessed HERA with a blinded trial against an expert on the stacking order of two multilayer graphene devices, an ablation of its knowledge base, and hundreds of offline replays in which models designed the trial's central measurement without running it. HERA's conclusions matched the expert's sealed conclusions on both tested devices. The ablation and the replay studies measured the impact of the knowledge base across different models, showing that with it every model designed better measurements and reasoned more soundly. To test how readily HERA extends to a new problem, we tasked it with finding and characterizing superconductivity in a different material system. This required a few hours of expert time to develop a new experimental protocol, after which HERA did both autonomously. These results show that, with a knowledge base designed for the task, language model agents are capable of sound experimental science.

Abstract: Quantum three-wave mixing is an important resource in superconducting quantum circuits. The traditional implementation uses Josephson junction arrays and is activated by magnetic flux. Here we introduce the Berry curvature dipole (BCD) quantum circuit element, which achieves quantum three-wave mixing without breaking time-reversal symmetry. This quantum three-wave mixing results from the BCD nonlinear Hall effect in noncentrosymmetric quantum materials taken to a dissipationless, quantum coherent few-photon regime. We show how nonlinear Hall materials can be integrated to superconducting microwave circuits to accomplish multiphoton vacuum Rabi splittings and dynamical stabilization of bosonic Schrödinger cat states. Such BCD circuit quantum electrodynamics are a concrete exemplar of a physical resource for quantum information processing enabled by nonlinear response in quantum materials.

Abstract: We investigate the thermally driven magnon spin current across a junction between a $d$-wave altermagnet (AM) and a ferromagnet (FM) or an antiferromagnet (AFM).
  The current consists of a component independent of the interface angle and an angularly dependent component with the $d$-wave form.
  For a sublattice-symmetric interface, the two junctions behave oppositely under reversal of the altermagnetic domain: in the FM/AM junction the angularly dependent component is odd and cancels on averaging over the domains, whereas in the AFM/AM junction the angularly independent component vanishes by symmetry and the angularly dependent component survives the domain average at a fixed Néel vector of the AFM.
  Both follow from a selective tunneling: a pair of spins bonded across the interface transmits magnons when the spins are parallel and not when antiparallel, which makes the AFM/AM junction a magnon valve: the configuration in which the bonded spins are antiparallel blocks the current up to the sublattice admixture of the magnons.
  The anisotropic tunneling is detectable as the sign reversal of the current between the interfaces normal to $[310]$ and $[130]$, and the valve reads the Néel vector of the altermagnet relative to that of the antiferromagnet through the magnitude of the current at a fixed interface.

The negatively charged silicon vacancy center in diamond (SiV-) is particularly favorable for strain-coupling to nanomechanical resonators because of its large spin-strain susceptibility and compatibility with device integration. Tensioned nanomechanical resonators offer an attractive route due to their ultra-long mechanical coherence times enabled by diluting defect-induced dissipation, though it is unclear if the same dilution effect undermines strain-mediated coupling to material defect centers. Thus, our work opens a pathway towards seconds-long coherence hybrid quantum systems utilizing point defects in diamond nanomechanics for quantum information processing.

Abstract: Higher Chern insulators host multiple dissipationless chiral edge states, one per unit of Chern number. We study the collective plasmon modes of these edge states at a domain wall between Chern numbers $\mathcal{C}$ and $-\mathcal{C}$, using a semiclassical model with Coulomb interactions. The plasmon dispersion is linear at long wavelengths, with a velocity that increases linearly with $\mathcal{C}$. As in conventional two-dimensional metals, the edge plasmon generates an elliptically polarized electric field that decays into the bulk and drives electrons in circular orbits, producing a static orbital magnetization (inverse Faraday effect). For topological edge plasmons, the magnetization has a nontrivial spatial profile set by two competing length scales: the finite extent of the edge states and the plasmon wavelength. At fixed edge-charge amplitude, it grows strongly with $\mathcal{C}$. At the domain wall, it approaches a finite plateau at low frequency and decays as $1/ω^2$ at high frequency. Its temperature dependence is nonmonotonic, with a maximum at an intermediate temperature. These results show how the Chern number controls plasmon-induced magnetization, in contrast to conventional edge plasmons.

Abstract: This chapter explores the critical role of emerging non-volatile memory (eNVM) devices in advancing neuromorphic computing. Inspired by the human brain's remarkable ability to perform complex tasks like pattern recognition and adaptation, neuromorphic computing offers immense potential for implementing complex neural networks (NNs) in real hardware. Despite ongoing research into the brain's complexities, its fundamental operation relies on neurons (processing units communicating via electrochemical signals) and synapses (junctions between neurons). The modifiable strength of the synapse is key to learning and memory functionality, allowing the brain to adapt and rewire itself. For dense and low-power circuits in neuromorphic computing, these eNVM devices are ideal due to their synaptic-like behavior and non-volatile information storage. Properties such as conductance modulation of these devices emulate synaptic plasticity, which is crucial for learning, and their scalability makes them promising for large-scale neuromorphic architectures. This chapter will delve into the various types of eNVM devices such as resistive RAM (ReRAM), phase-change memory (PCM), spintronics-based memory devices, and their applications in neuromorphic computing, discussing their challenges and opportunities, and highlighting recent advances in their integration within neuromorphic systems.

</details>

Abstract: Multiterminal Josephson junctions have recently attracted substantial attention. We find good agreement with semiclassical simulations for a single TI junction that take into account the top and bottom surface states. We observe pronounced non-convex shape deformations of the CCC under perpendicular magnetic fields that reflect the flux buildup in the loop formed by our trijunctions and provide a simple model sufficient to explain the observed effect. Abstract: Coupling quantum materials to electromagnetic cavities or resonators without external driving has gained prominence as a new control mechanism. These results identify superconductors with soft phase fluctuations as promising platforms for equilibrium control with terahertz resonators.

Abstract: We investigate magnon-photon interactions in a resonant cavity starting from the coupled Maxwell and Landau-Lifshitz equations linearized about an arbitrary micromagnetic ground state. By introducing an electric vector potential and a suitable interaction Lagrangian, the formulation avoids the cancellation of large magnetostatic contributions that arises in the conventional magnetic-vector-potential description and becomes inaccurate under modal truncation. The coupled system is diagonalized through a generalized Bogoliubov transformation, yielding canonical polariton variables that provide a natural basis for quantization. Application to a perpendicularly magnetized YIG disk in a rectangular microwave cavity shows hybridization between the cavity mode and several magnetostatic modes, together with appreciable cavity-wall-induced frequency shifts when the sample is placed close to the conducting walls.

Abstract: Time-reversal symmetry protects helical edge states against static nonmagnetic backscattering. We show that spatially uniform temporal modulation produces a time reflection-refraction crossover within the same quantum spin Hall phase while preserving time-reversal symmetry of the instantaneous Hamiltonian. A finite gap to the bulk and an edge splitting that vanishes at the Kramers crossing allow the modulation to suppress bulk excitation while remaining nonadiabatic between the edge channels. Virtual transitions through bulk states enable coherent conversion between counter-propagating edge channels at fixed crystal momentum. Near the crossing, $qT$ governs the leading crossover dynamics for a fixed ramp shape, where $q$ is the momentum offset and $T$ the ramp duration. Coherent interference can suppress and restore reflection as the ramp slows. In the adiabatic limit, two initially time-reversed wave packets follow their respective propagation branches and acquire equal geometric displacements. Their group-velocity contributions cancel, isolating the contribution from the Berry curvature to be directly observable. Temporal modulation thus controls protected states and quantum geometry along a topological edge.

Abstract: Van Hove (vH) singularities can enhance superconductivity by generating a large pairing susceptibility. Yet, it remains challenging to experimentally establish whether pairing really occurs near these points. We show that the saddle-point dispersion near a vH point gives rise to a `light-cone' vH-selective behavior of Friedel oscillations, which is drastically different from the familiar concentric circles. Each vH point maps onto its own real-space light-cone sector, allowing STM around a single impurity to measure whether---and how strongly---that saddle-point quasiparticle sector is gapped. A superconducting gap near vH points suppresses the oscillations in the directions corresponding to peaks in the gap function angular dependence, a behavior that provides a signature of vH-related pairing at STM-accessible length scales. This STM protocol, targeting vH superconductivity, is capable of determining whether superconducting pairing emerges near the vH points or elsewhere on the Fermi surface.

Abstract: First-principles Hamiltonians contain the electronic information needed to study moiré materials, but their size makes subsequent calculations costly. We present MoireKP, an automated workflow for constructing compact continuum models from localized-orbital ab initio Hamiltonians. The workflow constructs a valley-resolved truncated atomic-plane-wave (TAPW) Hamiltonian, selects a low-energy basis, and projects the crystal-symmetry operations into this basis. These representations determine the symmetry-allowed Hamiltonian terms, whose coefficients are obtained by projecting the low-energy effective Hamiltonian onto the corresponding operator basis. The model dimension, localized reference states, and harmonic cutoff can be selected automatically or specified by the user. This Hamiltonian-based construction retains wave-function information within the selected low-energy subspace. We apply the workflow to AA-stacked twisted bilayer MoTe$_2$, AB-stacked twisted bilayer ZrS$_2$, and A--AB twisted trilayer MoTe$_2$, covering K, M, and $Γ$ valleys. All models reproduce TAPW band energies within 3.0 meV in the target windows along high-symmetry $k$ paths.

Abstract: Planar energetics are the standard descriptors of twinnability in face-centred cubic metals and the usual validation targets for interatomic potentials. Here we show that they do not determine whether deformation twins persist in nanocrystalline aluminium. Two machine-learned potentials reproducing the same zero-load slip curves and closely spaced layered energy surfaces predict, from a common separated-fault precursor under identical loading, retained twin coverage of 64\% and 14\%. The divergence traces to forces within distorted partial cores acting along the trailing translation that would remove an outer fault. Planar energetics do set the margin: an outer fault more than halves the incremental barrier for middle-plane shear, yet twin completion and fault removal remain separated by only 10--21 mJ m\textsuperscript{-2}. Whether an assembled twin persists is decided instead by the orientation of local traction relative to the full-slip bisector and by the arrival order of partial-slip fronts from the grain-boundary network.

An analytical heat diffusion model is derived for predicting the FDTR phase response that incorporates a finite laser spot radius with a Gaussian intensity profile for the pump and probe lasers, as well as the dimensions of the nanoline grating. The model is validated by comparing with measurements of Au nanoline gratings, with widths ranging from 100 to 1000 nm, fabricated on a-SiO$_2$ and $c$-sapphire substrates. Due to the short phonon mean free paths (MFPs) in these substrates, thermal transport remains in the diffusive regime for the whole range of nanoline widths. The validated model will serve as a robust baseline for extending this method to measure effective thermal conductivity as a function of the heater size. This approach is particularly relevant for materials where non-diffusive thermal transport regimes enable probing phonon MFP dependent contributions to the thermal conductivity.

Instead, the heterostructures exhibit enhanced PL, reduced non-radiative recombination, and improved spatial uniformity of radiative QFLS compared with pristine 3D films. Under accelerated aging, this contrast becomes more pronounced: pristine films develop substantial spatial fluctuations in emission and QFLS, whereas capped heterostructures largely preserve their optoelectronic homogeneity. These findings indicate that the low-dimensional capping layer suppresses aging-induced optoelectronic disorder, demonstrating that electronic uniformity can be maintained despite morphological irregularity. This decoupling provides a design principle for developing durable, high-performance perovskite photovoltaics.

Abstract: Large-scale crystal databases have transformed materials discovery by making millions of structures and thermodynamic properties accessible to materials informatics. Yet dynamical stability remains sparsely characterized at a comparable scale because its evaluation is substantially more computationally demanding than conventional structure and energy calculations. This mismatch creates a critical gap between the rapidly expanding crystal-structure space and the physical information available for materials research and machine learning. Here we introduce Crystoria, a large-scale crystal dataset designed to bring dynamical stability to the scale of modern materials data. We construct Crystoria by systematically curating low-energy and metastable inorganic crystals, standardizing their structural identities, and combining machine-learning-accelerated structural relaxation with phonon-based dynamical screening. The resulting dataset contains 2,649,640 crystal structures, spanning 89 elements, 219 of the 230 space groups, and primitive cells containing 1-48 atoms. Among them, 1,793,353 structures (67.68%) are classified as dynamically stable, revealing substantial variation in dynamical stability across chemical and crystallographic space. By bringing dynamical stability to the scale of millions of crystal structures, Crystoria provides a physically grounded foundation for materials artificial intelligence (AI) and enables systematic exploration of the dynamical crystal landscape.

Abstract: Atomically thin magnets are governed by composition, thickness, stacking, strain, defects, substrates, ferroic order, and interfaces. This Review organizes two-dimensional (2D) magnetism using net magnetization and momentum-space spin splitting or spin-polarization patterns as primary coordinates, supplemented by sublattice structure and the symmetry relations between opposite-spin sublattices. Ferromagnets generally carry a net moment; ferrimagnets can be fully compensated. Conventional antiferromagnets may retain spin-degenerate bands, while altermagnets permit nonrelativistic alternating spin splitting under dimension-specific symmetry constraints. M1-M4 reporting classes introduced here distinguish layer-resolved atomically thin forms, non-layer-resolved thin flakes or films, bulk benchmarks, and theoretical candidates or design routes; the evidence category and ordering-temperature provenance are reported independently. Current experimental support for altermagnetism comes mainly from M2 thin-film and M3 bulk benchmarks, leaving a fully verified atomically thin M1 altermagnetic phase as an open experimental goal. Most proposed monolayer and few-layer systems therefore fall within M4, and contested cases clarify the measurements needed for phase assignment. Device readiness is compared qualitatively using operating temperature, readout, switching, domain control, stability, scalability, interface sensitivity, and integration difficulty. Reliable phase assignment and device assessment require complementary measurements from the same material form under matched conditions, ideally on the same specimen or in situ.

</details>

Abstract: Structural disorder can stabilize frameworks against pressure-induced failure, yet the ordered regions it contains limit this gain. We show that, in rigid-jointed frames under hydrostatic pressure, an ensemble containing arbitrarily large patches of a crystal at positive density, whose centres carry a scaled crystal prestress, cannot stay linearly stable once those patches are stressed beyond the crystal's threshold for localizable instabilities. A homogeneous loss of positive definiteness while all Bloch modes, including the acoustic limit, remain stable cannot localize, leaving a window for stabilization. Extending an acoustic criterion to prestress, we show for all point groups that such a window requires a strain that keeps the crystal's symmetry. Unless the patches are shielded from stress, disorder cannot protect a crystal against a failure that breaks its symmetry, and protects a cubic crystal at most against volume collapse. In two-dimensional Truchet frames, planted ordered domains bring the threshold within 1% of the bound, and correlations short of long-range order localize failure on ordered clusters. Across 876 slender-strut nets, 798 fail first by a localizable instability; the other 78 have a median window of 7.6%. Stability gained through disorder is capped by the localizable instabilities of the local order it permits.

</details>

Abstract: Transverse thermoelectric conversion, in which charge and heat currents are interconverted in orthogonal directions, has recently attracted increasing attention as an alternative to conventional longitudinal thermoelectric conversion. Although transverse Seebeck effect has been investigated in various materials, its reciprocal process, transverse Peltier effect, remains much less explored, and direct comparison of the two effects in the same homogeneous material is still limited. Here, we experimentally investigate both transverse Seebeck and Peltier effects in a single crystal of MoSi2, an axis-dependent conduction polarity material in which the sign of the Seebeck coefficient varies depending on the crystallographic axis. Multi-terminal voltage measurement determines the direction of the induced internal electric field and establishes the transverse Seebeck response. We also directly visualize the transverse temperature modulation consistent with the corresponding transverse Peltier effect by using lock-in thermography under an applied ac current. These results establish reciprocal transverse thermoelectric conversion in a homogeneous nonmagnetic single crystal without engineered interfaces.

</details>

Abstract: Achieving a large strain response to a small magnetic-field variation remains a challenge in magnetomechanical materials, particularly in topological systems where the coupling between spin reconstruction and lattice deformation remains unclear. Here, we report an anomalous magnetostrictive response associated with field-induced spin reorientation in the topological Kagome magnet TbMn6Sn6. Below its spin-reorientation temperature of 310 K, an in-plane magnetic field induces an abrupt increase in magnetostriction, which exceeds 120 ppm at 240 K. This transition produces a giant differential piezomagnetic coefficient of approximately 5000 ppm T-1, far exceeding the values reported for other topological magnetic materials and commercial FeGa alloys, and placing TbMn6Sn6 among the most field-sensitive magnetostrictive materials. The field position of this peak agrees with the anomalies in magnetization and magnetotransport, demonstrating that the large differential response is tied to field-induced spin reorientation. Thermal expansion also changes across the spin-reorientation boundary. Raman measurements reveal corresponding anomalies in phonon energy and linewidth, indicating that the reorientation modifies the lattice dynamics. The combined results support a mechanism in which competing magnetic anisotropies of the Tb and Mn sublattices, coupled through Tb-Mn exchange, create magnetic configurations with different equilibrium strains. This finding establishes spin reorientation as a route to giant piezomagnetic responses in topological magnets and provides a basis for magnetomechanical sensing and switching near room temperature.

Abstract: Lead zirconate (PbZrO3, PZO) is a prototypical antiferroelectric (AFE) system promising for applications such as energy storage and neuromorphic computing. However, in conventional thin-film architectures, its functional potential is often limited by substrate clamping. Although emerging freestanding membrane techniques offer an approach to release this clamping and enable dynamic strain engineering, a systematic understanding of how external strain modulates the switching between AFE and ferroelectric (FE) states is missing. In this study, we investigate the uniaxial strain effects on PZO membrane capacitors transferred onto flexible substrates via a gold-bonding method. We demonstrate that in-plane dynamic strain modulates the AFE-FE switching and functional properties. Corroborated by atomistic simulations, we reveal that the strain-dependent evolution arises from the superposition of the intrinsic strain dependence of specific domain orientation variants and extrinsic strain-induced redistribution of the relative population of different domain variants. These findings provide a predictive understanding of uniaxial strain tuning of AFE-FE switching in PZO, and pave the way for exploiting the functional potential of freestanding antiferroelectric membranes.

</details>

Devices based on an 80~nm TeO$_x$:Se active layer annealed for 9~h exhibit optimal performance, with broadband photocurrent generation from 300 to 1000~nm, yielding an open-circuit voltage of 0.50~V, a short-circuit current density of 8.50~mA~cm$^{-2}$, a fill factor of 0.44, and a maximum power conversion efficiency of approximately 1.87\%. These results establish selenium-alloyed TeO$_x$ as a viable oxide-derived absorber for thin-film photovoltaics and highlight chemical-state control as an effective route for improving photovoltaic performance.

The numerical results were confronted with the experimental values of $E$ and~$ν$. With these model assumptions, a very good agreement between numerical and experimental results was achieved. Finally, the experimental results were compared with classical analytical estimates. Among the analytical approaches, the Hashin-Shtrikman upper bound and self-consistent approximation reproduced the experimental Young's modulus with minor deviations.

Abstract: Realizing highly transparent and magnetic-field-resilient superconducting contacts is a critical bottleneck for the advancement of two-dimensional (2D) quantum devices. Molybdenum-rhenium (MoRe) alloys have been empirically favored as they uniquely yield ballistic, highly transparent contacts to graphene that survive in high-magnetic-field regimes; however, the microscopic origin of this anomalous transparency has remained a long-standing puzzle. Here, we resolve this paradox by revealing that pristine graphene/MoRe interfaces are governed by the classical A15 superconducting phase. Combining low-temperature transport, X-ray diffraction, and atomic-resolution electron microscopy, we demonstrate that as-deposited MoRe spontaneously forms the A15 phase in direct contact with graphene, whereas thermal annealing triggers a phase transition to a hexagonal close-packed (hcp) structure that severely degrades the Josephson transparency. Crucially, the one-dimensional (1D) transition-metal atomic chains unique to the A15 structure exhibit a near-perfect lattice match (mismatch < 1%) with graphene edges. First-principles calculations further prove that the d-orbitals of these historically renowned 1D chains possibly hybridize with graphene's Dirac cone near the K point. These findings not only uncover the structural and electronic origins of optimal MoRe contacts but also establish A15-phase superconductors as a geometrically ideal, universal platform for realizing robust quantum electronics based on 2D materials.

Abstract: Quantum materials exhibit diverse electronic and functional phenomena, but their characterization requires extensive experimental expertise, iterative decision-making and accumulated laboratory knowledge that is difficult to document and transfer. Here we introduce Quantum Agent (QUANTA), an agentic artificial intelligence (AI) framework for autonomous quantum-materials characterization. Using a widely adopted Physical Property Measurement System (PPMS) as an experimental platform, QUANTA combines retrieval augmentation using scientific literature, experimental protocols and prior measurements with specialized agents for experimental planning, troubleshooting, and data interpretation. A separate deterministic control layer validates instrument actions. Guided by incoming data, QUANTA evaluates measurement quality, revises experimental plans and selects subsequent measurements. We demonstrate these capabilities through transport measurements of the topological Weyl semimetal TaP, as well as investigations of nonlinear responses in magnetic material Co2MnGa. These results establish QUANTA as a generalizable framework for evidence-driven, safeguarded and closed-loop quantum-materials characterization.

</details>

Abstract: The continuous development of machine learning inter-atomic potentials (MLIAPs)has greatly accelerated materials research in recent years. However, the need to con- struct custom MLIAPs for each unique problem remains a bottleneck which raises the question of the transferability of MLIAPs. While foundation MLIAPs can achieve transferability due to the broad coverage of chemical space represented in their training sets, their size and computational cost limits their routine use. We address this gap by developing a lightweight general-purpose MLIAP for metal alloys spanning twelve elements (Al, Sc, Ti, V, Cu, Zn, Zr, Nb, Mo, Hf, Ta, W). Here, we utilize a step-wise active learning (AL) approach to develop a compact yet diverse dataset and model that generalizes well by interpolating from previously seen interactions. The resulting potentials transfer effectively to unseen, higher body-order alloys systems, requiring only unary and binary training data to capture ternary and quaternary systems. The model also transfers well to new elements via an alchemical extrapolation within the network, requiring only unary data for the new element and its alloys. These findings suggest that MLIAPs trained on targeted, diversity-driven datasets provide transferability at a relatively low computational cost, offering a practical path toward general-purpose, resource-efficient MLIAPs for materials modeling.

Abstract: Relaxor ferroelectrics are among the most important electromechanical materials owing to their exceptionally large electrostrictive responses. However, conventional relaxor behavior is generally associated with chemical complexity and structural disorder, where local compositional fluctuations generate polar nanoregions, making the microscopic origin of this behavior difficult to control and engineer. Here we report sliding relaxor ferroelectricity (S-RFE), a previously unknown relaxor state in which engineered stacking-phase heterogeneity enables cooperative interlayer sliding to generate collective relaxor behavior. Through stacking-phase engineering in rhombohedral boron nitride, we create sliding domains that act as structural analogues of polar nanoregions and cooperatively align under an electric field through interlayer sliding. This collective state produces reversible electromechanical deformation of up to approximately $-1.6\%$ with negligible hysteresis in approximately 300-nm-thick crystals, corresponding to approximately 1000 atomic layers. The resulting electrostriction coefficient ($Q_{33}$ of approximately $1200\,\mathrm{m}^{4}\mathrm{C}^{-2}$) exceeds those of most crystalline electrostrictors by orders of magnitude. Unlike conventional relaxors, whose electromechanical response is often suppressed by dipolar freezing at low temperatures, S-RFE remains active down to 1.6 K and maintains giant electromechanical functionality. Our results establish sliding relaxor ferroelectricity as a new route to relaxor behavior and reveal how atomic-scale interlayer motion can be amplified into collective mesoscale functionality across approximately one thousand layers, providing a materials-by-design platform for electromechanical, photonic, and quantum technologies based on van der Waals materials.

</details>

Abstract: Friction and wear of diamond are controlled by its surface chemistry, which determines whether covalent bonds form across the sliding interface. First-principles simulations have explored the role of surface orientation and passivation, but are limited to a few hundred atoms and tens of picoseconds. To bridge this gap, we develop a reactive machine learning interatomic potential for the C/H/N/O/Si chemical space, covering diamond, silicon nitride, silicon oxide, their surfaces, interfaces, and environmental molecules. The potential is trained on a large dataset of diverse structures and is tested on multiple physical properties, including ab initio reaction barriers on diamond surfaces, and structural/mechanical properties of amorphous Si$_3$N$_4$ and SiO$_2$. We then simulate sliding of diamond-Si$_3$N$_4$ interfaces with four surface orientations, clean and H/OH-passivated terminations, and normal loads from 0.1 to 23 GPa. We identify two different regimes: one where sliding is localized at the interface, and shear stress is proportional to the interfacial bond density, and another where shear is distributed inside the softer material, and adhesion due to full interfacial bonding dominates. These results show how passivation, surface orientation and load together determine interfacial bonding and friction, with implications for the environmental sensitivity of diamond wear. More broadly, the potential enables reactive simulations of diamond, silicon nitride and silica with near-ab initio accuracy at length and time scales beyond first-principles methods, providing a useful tool for future studies of wear at plowing asperities, chemical-mechanical polishing, and properties of amorphous ceramic films.

Phys. 56, 111901 (2015)] derivation, which Perdew et al. cite in support of their claim of a unique exchange-gradient coefficient, an integral of the form $\int_{-a}^{b}dx/x$ crosses a singularity and does not exist as an ordinary improper integral. Its finite Cauchy principal value (which is a particular way of regularizing the integral) is obtained only by excluding equal intervals on the two sides of the singularity; unequal exclusions give different answers. Independently, Sham's calculation with the screened interaction $e^{-λ_0 r}/r$ gives an exchange-gradient coefficient different from the bare-Coulomb calculations of Svendsen--von Barth [Int. J. Quantum Chem. 56, 351 (1995)] and Engel--Vosko [Phys. Rev. B 42, 4940 (1990)]. The discrepancy remains when $λ_0>0$ is so small that $1/λ_0$ is comparable to the size of the observable universe, making the two interactions experimentally indistinguishable on laboratory scales. Why should the exactly unscreened result be preferred, even if its calculation is accepted as valid? Our calculation shows that the ambiguity of the separate contributions cancels in the combined, regulator-independent coefficient $b_{xc}$.

This work reports the synthesis, phase transitions, and magnetotransport properties of the narrow-gap Zintl arsenide Eu$_2$CdAs$_2$. The compound undergoes antiferromagnetic ordering at $T_\mathrm{N} = 9$ K and exhibits semiconducting behavior with a low-temperature activation energy of 3.2 meV, achieving a large CMR effect of $-10^4\%$ at 9 T. Below $T_\mathrm{N}$, a distinct butterfly-shaped magnetoresistance hysteresis emerges without any macroscopic ferromagnetic order, revealing a transport anomaly likely dictated by AFM domain dynamics and/or the formation and percolation of localized magnetic polarons. Furthermore, a systematic survey of reported Eu-based Zintl phases uncovers a nearly exponential scaling trend between the maximum CMR magnitude and the low-temperature activation energy. Abstract: Chiral spacer cations crystallize two-dimensional hybrid perovskites in the polar Sohncke group $P2_1$, which places the polar axis in the inorganic layer. We analyze the spin-orbit field that this geometry allows and test it against first-principles spin textures of ten polar chiral lead-halide perovskites, their mirror enantiomers and four halide-substituted analogues. The point group $C_2$ with the twofold axis in the layer forbids the helical Rashba field and allows a linear term $γ_1 k_x σ_z$, the Rashba interaction of the in-plane polar axis, together with chirality-related Dresselhaus and radial terms. Thirty-four two-band $k \cdot p$ models were fitted to each band pair and ranked by the agreement of their spin directions with the calculation rather than by the band energies, which cannot distinguish them. In all strongly polar compounds, the conduction band is described by the persistent term alone, with $γ_1 = 1.09$-$1.98$ eV Å and a spin agreement of at least 0.98, whereas the best Rashba or Dresselhaus model reaches 0.20. The $R$ and $S$ enantiomers of each compound have opposite polarizations, and therefore opposite $γ_1$ and opposite persistent spin textures of equal magnitude, as required by the mirror that relates them; this holds in all ten compounds, so the choice of the enantiomer selects the spin direction. The magnitude of $γ_1$ correlates with the disparity of the in-plane Pb-X-Pb angles ($r = 0.95$). With electron masses of $0.26$-$0.43\,m_e$ along the spin-splitting directions, the associated persistent spin helices have wavelengths of 3.4-8.2 nm.

Abstract: The low-energy nuclear isomeric transition of $^{229}$Th is a promising frequency reference for a solid-state nuclear optical clock. Wide-band-gap fluoride crystals such as CaF$_2$ offer transparent hosts for embedding Th nuclei at high density. Substitution of Th$^{4+}$ for Ca$^{2+}$ requires charge compensation, linking the local defect structure to nuclear quadrupole splitting. We use first-principles calculations to examine neutral Th dimers compensated by four interstitial F ions ($\mathrm{F}_{i}^{-}$). Screening different compensation geometries by their relative formation energies identifies a family of low-energy configurations with closely similar local structures. These configurations share large electric field gradients and small asymmetry parameters, consistent with the experimental high-EFG site 3. Their calculated quadrupole splittings are further compared with the measured C13 spectra. Together, the energetic and spectroscopic results support $\mathrm{F}_{i}^{-}$-compensated Th dimers as candidate microscopic structures for site 3 in Th:CaF$_2$.

Abstract: The unstable planar fault energies (UPFEs) govern slip and twinning but are not experimentally measurable. Using EAM potentials complemented by DFT, we investigate their temperature and compositional dependence and their relationship with the shear modulus. For pure \textit{fcc} elements with low stacking fault energies (SFEs), UPFEs are substantially less temperature-sensitive than SFEs. The reported linear relationship between UPFEs and the product of shear modulus and Burgers vector ($μb_{\mathrm{p}}$) holds for close-packed elements and solid solutions, but deviates for intermetallics and non-close-packed elements. In the Co-Cr-Fe-Mn-Ni system, binary alloys show composition-dependent deviations, while the correlation strengthens with increasing chemical complexity. Furthermore, $μb_{\mathrm{p}}$ of multicomponent alloys linearly correlates with the composition-weighted elemental $μb_{\mathrm{p}}$ ($R^2=0.95$), despite a systematic negative deviation. This provides a simple, computationally inexpensive route for estimating $μb_{\mathrm{p}}$ and screening UPFEs directly from pure elemental constituents.

Abstract: Large and electrically controllable spin splitting in nonmagnetic materials is highly desirable for condensed-matter physics and spintronics, enabling strongly spin-polarized quasiparticles and unconventional spin transport as well as their electrical control. Here, we establish a general framework for critical spin-polarized Weyl fermions in two-dimensional (2D) nonmagnetic systems. We predict the $α$-Bi-like Group-V puckered-layer family of 2D ferroelectrics as a material platform combining critical spin-Weyl fermions, giant spin splitting, and electrically switchable nonlinear and spin transport. Our calculations reveal exchange-scale spin splitting induced by spin-orbit coupling and a $p$-wave spin texture in the BiAs monolayer. Moreover, BiAs hosts four critical spin-Weyl points, each formed within a single-spin channel. The nearly quantized $\langle S_z\rangle$ is protected by an emergent $S_z$-conservation symmetry in the low-energy limit. The spin-Weyl cones also give rise to unconventional spin-transport phenomena, including a purely out-of-plane Edelstein response and a pronounced spin Nernst effect. Ferroelectric reversal further enables electrical switching of the $p$-wave spin texture and inversion-odd responses, while the tunability of these properties through composition, strain, and magnetic field is also systematically demonstrated across the entire family. These results establish both a general nonmagnetic framework and a promising material platform for exploring critical spin-Weyl physics, nonlinear Hall phenomena, and unconventional spin transport.

Abstract: Paramagnetic color centers in silicon are being investigated recently for use in quantum technologies. While the origin of most of the prominent photoluminescence (PL) lines are well understood, that is not the case for those connected to hydrogen, even though it is almost always present in silicon, e.g., shallow hydrogen-related donors (SHDs), but their atomic structure remains elusive. Recent experiments suggested connection between the SHDs and the so-called X and the B$_i$ (or X$_i$) PL-lines. An SHD, termed the MT2 center, was tentatively assigned to a bistable, negative-U defect, arising due to the interaction of hydrogen with the self-interstitial complex I$_4$. Using high-level density functional theory calculations, we investigate the interaction of hydrogen with self-interstitial complexes In. A shallow effective-mass-theory donor arises when hydrogen attacks a Si-Si bond in I$_n$, inserting itself as a bond-center interstitial (I$_n$H$_{BC}$) in an exothermic reaction. We demonstrate this in the case of a single self-interstitial and of a complex of four. The latter I$_\mathrm{4}$H$_\mathrm{BC}$ complex shows electronic, spin and PL properties remarkably close to MT2. We find that hydrogen in the presence of I$_4$ follows the same behavior as in pristine silicon, i.e., it is a negative-U bistable defect, only detectable under illumination. In the I$_4$H$_{BC}$ complex, H can hop via photo-assisted mechanism between equivalent BC sites of the I$_4$ complex, leading to a motional averaged tetragonal symmetry as experimentally observed for MT2. The zero-phonon line is distinct from the X-line (attributed to the bare I$_4$ complex), and falls well into the range of the X$_i$ lines. Our results identify the I$_4$H complex as a prototype for a class of SHDs, inferring that similar SHDs may arise, with the X$_i$ lines as their PL fingerprints, when H attacks I$_n$ clusters.

</details>

Abstract: Driving quantum materials with light can reshape not only their electronic band structures but also the geometry of their electronic wavefunctions. In graphene, circularly polarized light is predicted to open gaps at the Dirac points and induce a pseudospin texture and Berry curvature characteristic of the Haldane model, realizing a Floquet Chern insulator. Transport and all-optical measurements have provided evidence for a light-induced anomalous Hall response, but cannot resolve the underlying electronic states in both energy and momentum space. Time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy has revealed Floquet-Bloch sidebands and hybridization gaps at crossings between Floquet bands. However, these experiments used linearly polarized driving, which preserves time-reversal symmetry and thus does not induce a topological phase. Here, we combine circular-dichroic time-resolved extreme-ultraviolet momentum microscopy with ab initio theory to investigate graphene driven by circularly polarized light. We observe a pump-induced photoemission circular dichroism near the Dirac point that reverses sign with pump helicity and across the Dirac energy, in agreement with our calculations. These results link the measured dichroic response to the light-induced pseudospin texture and its associated Berry curvature, providing spectroscopic evidence for the Floquet-engineered topological insulating state in graphene.
